# REVIEW-057 round one — the phase 1 publication plan, before any step runs

You are an adversarial reviewer. The subject is a plan to publish a private
research repository as a public one. The owner has ruled every decision in it,
and asked for this review **before any step runs**. Publication is not
reversible: anything pushed to a public GitHub repository can be cloned, cached
and indexed within minutes. Your job is to find what in the plan is false, what
would leak, what would fail, and what the plan has not priced. Agreement is not
useful to the owner. A finding that the plan survives is useful only if you
tried to break it and say how.

## STEP 0 — do this before anything else, and report the results first

1. Run `curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' https://github.com` and
   `curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' https://registry.npmjs.org`. Print
   both codes. Run `gh auth status 2>&1 | head -3` and say whether `gh` is
   authenticated. If network fails, print `NO NETWORK` and continue without it.
2. Run `pwd` and `git log --oneline -1`. The working copy is a clone at commit
   `adee1f0`. Work only inside this clone.
3. **Do not read** any directory outside this clone, and in particular: any path
   under `~/.claude`, `~/.codex`, `~/.wconf`, `/tmp/wconf`, any `scratchpad`
   directory, and the repository this clone was made from. `~/.wconf` and
   `/tmp/wconf` hold real (Devnet, no-value) secrets. **Never print, cat or copy
   them.** You do not need them: do not run `make check-no-secrets` without
   `--no-local-store`, and do not run `cargo`, `make check-zeroize-bounds` or
   `tools/rebuild-env.sh`.
4. You may write scratch files under `$TMPDIR`. Do not commit, push, create,
   rename or edit anything on GitHub. **Read-only `gh` calls only.**
5. ⚠ **Your transcript is committed to this repository after redaction.** Two
   sources must never appear in it as text:
   - **The private repository's issue bodies and comments.** You may query
     `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions` with `gh issue list` / `gh issue view`
     **only through `--json … --jq` that prints numbers, states, dates, counts,
     titles and `U-nn` ids**. Never print a body or a comment, or any sentence
     from one.
   - **Withheld rows.** The file `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md` is
     gitignored and is not in your clone. Its row ids are listed under seam 3.
     Do not try to obtain its contents. ⚠ **Your clone's history still holds
     them**: `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` carried those rows until they were withheld.
     Do not run `git log -p`, `git show`, `git blame` or `git diff` on
     `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md` at any commit other than `adee1f0`.

### Seam 2 — D4: the rename, the local folder, and the pre-push guard

The ruling keeps the local folder named `xrpl-confidential-positions` while its
`origin` becomes `xrpl-confidential-positions-private`, and adds a `pre-push`
hook that refuses any URL naming `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions` without

repository.

`pre-push` — as typed, after `url.<base>.insteadOf`, after `pushurl`? Which pushes
skip the hook: `--no-verify`, `gh repo create --source . --push`, `gh repo sync`,
a fresh clone, a second worktree, `core.hooksPath`, a push from an IDE? Is a
regular expression over the URL enough when GitHub accepts
`git@github.com:nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions`,
`https://github.com/NANDANITO/xrpl-confidential-positions.git`, trailing slashes
and `ssh://` forms? **Name any route by which the private history reaches the
public repository that the plan does not close.** Also check GitHub's rename
behaviour as the request quotes it, against GitHub's current docs.

# Decision request — phase 1 publication

- **Date:** 2026-10-18
- **For:** the project owner
- **Plan:** `docs/PLAN-PUBLIC-LAUNCH-2026-09-22.md`, phase 1
- **Status:** phase 1 items 1 to 4 are built on four stacked branches. Item 5, the
  publication itself, and six decisions are yours. **Nothing has been published,
  pushed, force-pushed or merged to `main`, and no history has been rewritten.**
- **Ruled 2026-10-18 by the owner:** the recommended option for D1, D2, D5 and D6.
  D3 is not to ask upstream for a licence, and its option is open. D4 is open.
- **Revised the evening of 2026-10-18**, after the facts behind D3 and D4 were
  re-measured: D3's context was wrong in three places and now carries a
  recommendation; D4 carries one too; **D7 is new**; and the publication steps
  carry all three.
- **Ruled later on 2026-10-18 by the owner: the recommended option for D3 (A′),
  D4 and D7 (A).** Every decision is now ruled. The owner also ruled that the local
  checkout keeps its folder name (see D4), and asked for an adversarial review of
  this document before any step runs.

## What is ready

The four branches form one stack, each cut from the one before. Merging
`decision/phase-1-publication` merges all of them.

| branch | phase 1 item | state |

at `dfb650e` was exported with `git archive` into a fresh single-commit
repository, and the gate ran on that exact commit with the local store loaded:

```text
controls: 93/93 passed, run through the same scanRepo() as the real check

## The decisions

### D1. A third review round on the secret sweep

**Context.** `REVIEW-056d` set a stopping condition before round two ran: no third
round unless round two found a fix that did not discharge its finding. It found
three. The precedent for that case, `REVIEW-049` and E-311, gave the third round
to you with a recommendation.

**Ruled A, and DONE 2026-10-18 (`REVIEW-056e`).** Round three found 9 findings, no
HIGH; 8 upheld and 1 upheld in part, all fixed; the rotation is closed and its fixes
are unreviewed, as ruled.

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A. One more leg, on the round-two fixes only, and close after it whatever it finds** *(recommended; ruled)* | about 45 minutes of a Codex leg and an hour of adjudication | the gate that decides publication has had its latest fixes seen |
| B. Close at two | nothing | round two found no HIGH, and its findings moved from coverage to the record's sentences: the convergence pattern `CLAUDE.md` describes |

### D2. The revision log in a squashed repository

**Context, measured.** `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS.md` is derived from git history, and
`make check-claims-revisions` fails it if a commit it names is not an ancestor.
In the rehearsal repository it failed: *"db2fd12 is not an ancestor of HEAD"*.
`make ci` runs that gate, so **the public repository's first CI run would fail.**

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Keep the archive's log as a dated, static file, and regenerate a fresh log from the public history** *(recommended; ruled)* | ⚠ priced here as *"a rename and one regeneration"*, and that was wrong: the builder refused to run in a squashed export, because its oracle checks three curated commits that only the private history has. **Built after the ruling**: the oracle skips only when none of its commits exists and `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS-ARCHIVE.md` is present. Rehearsed, and `make ci` then passed every step in the export | the per-row dating survives as a record, and the gate keeps protecting the register going forward |
| B. Keep the archive's log and drop the gate from public CI | one line in `tools/ci.sh` | the record survives; nothing in public fails when a register row disappears |
| C. Publish the history instead of squashing | reverses the E-309 ruling, and history carries 1,045 personal-path hits that `--strict-history` fails, so it needs a history rewrite first | the commit citations in the register resolve |

### D3. The licence, and the vendored `mpt-crypto` patch

**Context, searched on 2026-10-18 and re-measured that evening.** This repository is
Apache-2.0. It publishes `tools/spike-2026-08-18/wconf-nonces.patch`, 1,657 lines
against `XRPLF/mpt-crypto` at `85dbac7`, across six files under `src/`.

⚠ **This said *"with 341 context lines of upstream source"*, and that undercounted
it.** The patch's 491 removed lines are upstream source too, so it carries about
832 upstream lines verbatim. ⚠ **A second file was not priced at all.**
`tools/mpt-crypto-bench/wconf_bench.c` says its setup code is *"lifted from the
corresponding upstream test"*, and 70 of its 217 substantive lines appear verbatim
in upstream's `tests/` at the pin. Some of those 70 are API boilerplate. The
search covered the patch and every tracked `.c` and `.h` file, and nothing else.

**That C repository carries no licence that these searches found:** `gh api repos/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/license`
returns 404, the root listing has no licence file at the pin or at `main`,
`conanfile.py` declares none, and no file under `src/` or `include/` has an SPDX
line, a licence text or a copyright line. No issue or pull request in it mentions
a licence; a control query in the same search returned hits, so the empty result
is real.

⚠ **The npm package's ISC licence is not evidence about the C source.**
`@xrplf/mpt-crypto@0.1.0` names `XRPLF/xrpl.js` as its repository, and ships
TypeScript and a WASM build of `mpt-crypto` 1.0.4 with no C source. Its `LICENSE`
is an ISC notice with the line *"Copyright (c) 2015 Ripple Labs Inc."*. Option A as
first written cited it as covering the C code, which is a substituted source.

⚠ **Nothing in the tree carves the upstream lines out of Apache-2.0.** `LICENSE`
is the only licence file and there is no `NOTICE`, so the publication as built
would appear to license Ripple's code under Apache-2.0.

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A′. Publish the patch and the harness, with a `NOTICE` that carves them out** *(recommended; ruled)* | one `NOTICE` file and one README line, committed before the export | a stranger can rebuild the signer, and the tree stops appearing to relicense upstream code. ⚠ The redistribution itself stays unlicensed, and is accepted by name |
| A. Publish the patch, citing the npm package's ISC licence | none now | ⚠ **not recommended**: it rests on a grant nothing states |
| B. Ask upstream to add a licence file first, then A | an upstream filing and its wait | **ruled out by the owner, 2026-10-18** |
| C. Publish without the patch or the lifted harness | the signer becomes buildable only from the private archive | no upstream code is republished; **the fallback if the copyright holder objects** |
| D. Move the patch to a GitHub fork of `mpt-crypto` | rework `tools/rebuild-env.sh` and `make vendor-verify`, and rebuild the signer, just before publication | the copy rests on GitHub's forking grant. ⚠ It does not settle modification: *"Although a code host such as GitHub may allow you to view and fork the code, this does not imply that you are permitted to use, modify, or share the software"* (choosealicense.com, read 2026-10-18) |

**Why A′ over C.** The signer is the main thing this project built, and its point
is that a claim is checked by running it. Under C a stranger cannot build it. The
practical risk under A′ is low: the code is published openly by the organisation
whose surrounding stack is ISC, and this project's use of it sends findings back
upstream. A′ cannot make the copy licensed. What it does is stop this repository
from mislabelling someone else's code, which is the part of the risk this project
controls.

**The `NOTICE`, drafted** on the pattern `evm-confidential-positions` already uses:

```text
xrpl-confidential-positions
Copyright 2026 Nandan

Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (see LICENSE).

This repository includes material from a third party that is NOT covered by
that licence:

- tools/spike-2026-08-18/wconf-nonces.patch is a patch against XRPLF/mpt-crypto
  at 85dbac7. Its context lines and removed lines, about 832, are upstream source.
- tools/mpt-crypto-bench/wconf_bench.c lifts proof setup code from mpt-crypto's
  tests at the same commit.

XRPLF/mpt-crypto carried no licence file at that commit or at main when checked
on 2026-10-18. The npm package @xrplf/mpt-crypto is ISC-licensed; it ships a WASM
build and no C source, and this repository does not treat its licence as a grant
for the C source. The upstream lines are not relicensed here. They are
reproduced so that the signer can be rebuilt, and they will be removed if the
copyright holder asks.
```

Plan item 6, attribution, also asks that every third-party citation name its
upstream and that P-7's credits be right on every public surface. **That check
was not run this session**, beyond the README's credit section, which now names
all three audits.

### D4. The public repository's name and owner

**Recommended and ruled: the public repository is `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions`,
and the private one is renamed `xrpl-confidential-positions-private` first.** That
is the pattern the EVM study already uses. `nandanito/evm-confidential-positions-private`
is private and `nandanito/evm-confidential-positions` is public, created the next
day (both read with `gh repo view` on 2026-10-18). The private repository holds the
name today, so the name has to be freed before the public one can take it. The
personal account matches the blog and the LinkedIn post. An organisation adds
nothing for a single-maintainer research artifact.

**The rename, read on GitHub's rename page on 2026-10-18.** After a rename, *"all
existing information, with the exception of project site URLs, is automatically
redirected to the new name."* If a new repository then takes the old name,
*"redirects to the renamed repository will no longer work."* Here that is what is
wanted: the canonical name should land on the public repository. Every existing
clone needs `git remote set-url origin` to the new name. The page also says
*"GitHub will not redirect calls to an action hosted by a renamed repository"*;
this repository hosts no action.

**Ruled, with one addition: the local folder keeps its name.** This checkout stays
at `…/xrpl-confidential-positions`. Claude Code's memory is keyed by the folder's
path, and the owner found that renaming the EVM study's folder broke both its Claude
Code and its Codex sessions. Only its `origin` changes, to `xrpl-confidential-positions-private`. The
public repository's working copy is a separate folder, the step 4 export.

⚠ **That leaves a folder whose name matches the PUBLIC repository holding the
PRIVATE history**, and one mistake publishes all of it: the history that
`--strict-history` fails on 1,045 personal-path hits, and the 36 commits behind the
withheld rows. Once the public repository takes the old name, a stale `origin` in
any clone stops redirecting to the archive and points at the public repository
instead. **So step 8 also installs a `pre-push` hook in every private clone that
refuses any remote URL naming `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions` without the
`-private` suffix**, and the public URL is never added as a remote here.

The README's footnote records `xrpl-confidential-positions` as held since
2026-08-16, which stays true. The five-minute path says *"from a fresh clone"* and
names no URL, so nothing else changes.

### D5. Whether the vocabulary translation reaches the records

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Leave `docs/reviews/`, `docs/decisions/` and `CLAIMS.md` as written, under the banner** *(recommended, and what is built)* | none | the records keep their value as a record of what was believed |
| B. Translate them in the published tree only | a scripted pass at publication | a public reader never meets the shorthand; the published records then differ from the archive's |

### D6. Whether CI runs the signer's tests

**Context.** The signer links a patched `mpt-crypto` that `tools/rebuild-env.sh`
builds with Homebrew `openssl@3`, a macOS build. `make ci` therefore skips
`cargo test`, `check-test-support` and `check-zeroize-bounds`, says so on every
run, and reports five signer-starting facility tests as skipped rather than passed.

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Local-only, as built** *(recommended for publication)* | none | an honest CI that names what it does not run |
| B. A macOS runner job: `tools/rebuild-env.sh` then `cargo test` | unverified; pinned GitHub clones at every run; its own verification round | the signer is tested on every pull request |
| C. Port the vendored build to Linux | rework of `build.sh` and `rebuild-env.sh` | the same, on the cheaper runner |

### D7. Copying the issues, so the numbers the documents cite resolve

**Context, measured on 2026-10-18.** The README says bare issue numbers are issues
on *this* repository. `git grep` finds the pattern `issue #N` in 119 files, most of
them under `docs/reviews/` and `docs/decisions/`. Every number from #1 to #11
appears, and #13; #1 appears 157 times, #10 70 times and #11 50 times. That counts
the pattern, and a few hits are upstream issues rather than this repository's.
These numbers belong to the private repository, which has 13 issues, three of them open (#5, #8, #10), 17
comments between them and no pull requests. A fresh public repository numbers from
#1, and issues and pull requests share one counter, so every citation would point
at the wrong issue or at nothing.

⚠ **Six of the 13 cite withheld rows, and some restate them.** The bodies of #6
(U-28), #7 (U-31) and #10 (U-32), and comments on #2 (U-36), #3 (U-37) and #4
(U-35, U-38), cite rows that are in `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md` and not
in `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md`. #10's body also restates what U-32 says. **Copying them
verbatim would publish part of what E-240 withheld.**

| option | cost | what it buys |
|---|---|---|
| **A. Title-only stubs for the ten closed issues, and full copies of the three open ones** *(recommended; ruled)* | 13 issues created in order, with #10's U-32 passage redacted and every body run through the secret sweep first | every cited number resolves, and no withheld row id leaves, because #10 is the only one of the open three that cites one. ⚠ The scan matched row ids, so the three open bodies still need a read for restated content. The closed issues' outcomes are already in the published register and reviews |
| B. Full copies of all 13 with their comments, after a redaction pass you approve | six issues redacted by hand and 17 comments copied | the discussions themselves become public |
| C. No copies. The step 6 sentence also says issue numbers refer to the archive, and the open three are re-filed under new numbers | the citations to #8 and #10 edited | ⚠ the public repository's own #1 to #13 would then collide with every cited number |

A stub carries the original title and says: *"A stub. This was issue #N in the
private archive, opened DATE and closed DATE. Its discussion stays there; where
this repository cites issue #N, the outcome is in the document that cites it."*
⚠ **The issues have to be created before anything else can take a number**, so
the public repository is created private and made public only after them.

## Item 5, the publication steps — every decision ruled 2026-10-18

1. **Apply D3**: add `NOTICE` beside `LICENSE` as drafted under D3, and a line in
   the README's *Licence* section pointing to it. Commit on the stack and re-run
   `make ci`.
2. Merge the stack to `main` and push it to the private origin.
3. ~~Run D1's round, if ruled, and fix what it upholds.~~ **Done 2026-10-18**
   (`REVIEW-056e`).
4. Export the tree with `git archive` into a fresh repository and commit it as
   one commit. Confirm with `git ls-files` that `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md`
   and `docs/evidence/f12-emsdk-2026-10-18/logs-unscrubbed/` are absent, and that
   `NOTICE` is present.
5. Apply D2: copy `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS.md` to `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS-ARCHIVE.md`,
   commit, then run `bun run tools/build-claims-revisions.ts` and commit the
   regenerated log. D5 needs nothing: the records keep their wording.
6. Add to the README, in its first screen: *"Public history starts at
   publication. This repository was developed privately from 2026-08-16 and
   published as one commit on DATE; the commit hashes cited in `CLAIMS.md` and
   `docs/reviews/` refer to that private archive and do not resolve here."* Under
   D7's option A, add: *"Issues #1 to #13 were copied from the archive in order, so
   the issue numbers cited here resolve; the ten closed ones are stubs."*
7. **Run the phase 1 gate on that exact commit**, with the local store:
   `bun run tools/check-no-secrets.ts --repo <public-clone> --rev HEAD`.
8. **Apply D4**: rename the private repository to `xrpl-confidential-positions-private`,
   run `git remote set-url origin` in every clone, and confirm with `git ls-remote origin`.
   Install the `pre-push` guard in every private clone, and prove it refuses by
   pushing to the public URL before that repository exists.
9. Create `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions` **as a private repository** and
   push the one commit. **Apply D7 before anything else can take a number**: put
   the 13 bodies in a scratch repository and run the sweep over it, create the
   issues in order, close the ten, and confirm with `gh issue list` that the numbers
   match. Then make it public, and set: private vulnerability reporting on, which
   `SECURITY.md` and `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md` rely on; branch protection requiring the
   `ci` check; and, recommended, the workflow's actions pinned by commit SHA rather
   than tag.
10. **Watch the first CI run.** The workflow has never run on GitHub; `tools/ci.sh`
    has run only on macOS, in this checkout and in a fresh clone.

## Found this session, and not in the plan

- **The Devnet evidence the README offers a stranger is no longer served** by the
  public endpoint, whose history now starts at ledger 5,171,980. Devnet was not
  reset (E-319). Phase 2's hosted demo should not depend on old transaction
  hashes either.
- **Devnet runs rippled 3.4.1**, not the `3.4.0-rc5` the session prompt recorded.
- **The F-12 build logs were never committed**, so the README beside them cited
  evidence a reader could not open. They are committed now, scrubbed (E-317).
- **The licence question in D3** is not in the plan, which lists licence terms as
  open without saying why they might be hard.
- **D3's first pricing undercounted the upstream code by more than half**, because
  a patch's removed lines are upstream source as well as its context lines.
- **The issue numbers were a publication blocker nobody had listed** (D7). The
  squash ruling covered commit hashes and not issue numbers, which also start
  again in a new repository.

 succeeded in 0ms:
# PLAN — public repository, hosted demo, and the two posts

**Status:** the shape is ruled (`CLAIMS.md` E-309, 2026-09-22). Nothing in phases 1
to 3 starts before phase 0's exit criterion is met. **This is a plan, not an
authorisation:** each phase names the gate it must pass, and two of them require an
adversarial rotation before any code is written.

**The four goals, in the owner's words:** make the repository public and
contributable; deploy a frontend to Vercel so people can try it; a LinkedIn post
answering *"is confidential transfer possible on XRPL?"*; a blog post on
`nandan.me/writing` covering the project.

**What was ruled on 2026-09-22**, four decisions, each taken on the recommendation:

| Decision | Ruling |
|---|---|
| Demo scope | **Read-only.** No hosted signer, no keys in the page |
| History | **Squashed at publication.** This repository stays the archive of record |
| Filing gate | **The four findings are filed first.** B-28's own condition |
| Repo scope | **Everything but `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md`**, `docs/reviews/` included |

---

## Phase 0 — close the upstream loop

**Why it is first:** B-28 says the repository stays private until the unfiled
findings are filed or recorded as not-to-file. Eight are recorded (E-298, E-302,
E-308). Four are ruled to file and none is drafted. Publishing a register that names
defects in another team's code, by file and line, before that team has been told is
the thing `docs/UPSTREAM-FILING-PROTOCOL.md` exists to prevent.

1. ~~**The external review of `CLAIMS.md` E-296 to E-307**, ruled in E-308.~~
   **DONE 2026-09-22 — `REVIEW-048`, `CLAIMS.md` E-310.** Three differed legs,
   seventeen findings upheld, all fixed in the rows. Two of them change what gets
   filed (below), and one added **B-30**: the register had settled *"has upstream
   already raised this?"* against Trail of Bits alone, and a second audit of the same
   code exists and is not publicly obtainable.
2. **Round two on `REVIEW-048`'s fixes.** Added 2026-09-22. `CLAUDE.md`'s two-round
   rule — the second round reviews the fixes — and it is owed because round one
   found defects in the rows. **Its stopping condition, set before it runs: no
   third round unless round two finds a fix that did not discharge its finding.**
   **DONE 2026-09-22 — `REVIEW-049`, `CLAIMS.md` E-311, and THE CONDITION FIRED.**
   Three of its eight findings were undischarged, all of them surviving copies or
   short lists, the worst being that B-30's list missed the two filings already sent.
   All eight are fixed. **A third round is owed and running it is the owner's call:**
   recommended as one corpus leg on the native light pass, asking only whether any
   corrected sentence has a surviving copy, **with the rotation closing after it
   whatever it finds.**
   **Ruled and DONE 2026-09-22 — `REVIEW-050`, `CLAIMS.md` E-312. The rotation is
   closed.** Five more surviving copies, all fixed, the worst being the README's
   front door naming one audit.
3. **Draft F-9, F-12, F-13 and F-17.** `/upstream-issue`, then `/issue-review`, then
   the owner's sign-off on each final text. All four go to `XRPLF/mpt-crypto`.
   Each draft states how its finding was established — F-9 run, F-12 run at
   `85dbac7` and read at `1.0.5`, F-13 and F-17 read at `1.0.5`. Four instructions
   from `REVIEW-048`:
   - **F-9** leads with the substitution: rippled's `init.sh` was not run, only its
     remote-add was reproduced by hand.
   - **F-12** says in its first paragraph that upstream's WASM job is green on `main`
     at the pinned emsdk, and **its remedy is three link lines, not the two its
     register row names** (`build-wasm.sh:363`, `:365`, `:374`).
   - **F-13** shows the component table, not the totals. Of two reviewers who re-derived
     1276 B and 1210 B from the row, one got the composition wrong.
   - **F-17 changed shape.** It may not claim the paper's nonce input omits
     `TransactionContextID`: Remark 6.1 never defines `statement`, §6.2 uses the
     word to include the context, and upstream's prover calls its variable
     `stmt_hash`. It files the ambiguity and its consequence.
4. **Search B-30's corpus before sending**, and record the search in each draft.
   `docs/UPSTREAM-FILING-PROTOCOL.md` rule 4. The reachable Sherlock-citing issues
   touch none of these four subjects, so this is unlikely to move them; it still has
   to be recorded rather than assumed.
   **DONE 2026-09-22 — `CLAIMS.md` E-313, `REVIEW-051`.** *Attackathon* widened the corpus to 104
   items, plus the 16 PRs against the contest's public code branch. None touches the four subjects.
5. **File them, and close issue #6** when F-12 is filed. **F-9 FILED 2026-09-28 as `mpt-crypto#148`** (E-314). **F-17, F-12 and F-13 FILED 2026-10-18 as `mpt-crypto#153`, `#154` and `#155`** (E-315). All four are filed.

**Exit:** four filings live at upstream, or a recorded ruling deferring one by name. **Met 2026-10-18.**

---

## Phase 1 — the public repository

**Gate:** phase 0's exit, plus the secret sweep in 1.1 passing on the exact tree that
will be published.

1. **Secret sweep, over the publication tree and over history.** `/tmp/wconf/state.json`
   was never tracked, and that is the claim to verify rather than assume: account
   seeds, ElGamal secrets, `WCONF_ELGAMAL_SK` values, personal addresses and any
   `~/.wconf` path. Write it as a gate (`make check-no-secrets`) with a control that
   must fail, because a sweep recorded in prose is a claim with no falsifier.
2. **Vocabulary.** `tapFailHard` is this project's invention and appears across the
   tree; `docs/VOCABULARY.md` carries the translation. Translate every
   **reader-facing** surface — `README.md`, `GETTING-STARTED-CLAUDE-CODE.md`, the
   architecture document, the web copy, both posts. `docs/reviews/` is a historical
   record and keeps its wording under a banner that points at the translation.
   ⚠ This shorthand escaped into a public upstream filing once already (E-156).
3. **The front door for a stranger.** `README.md` rewritten around what the artifact
   proves, what it refuses to claim (`CLAIMS.md` P-1 to P-12, P-4 and P-9 especially),
   and a five-minute path to running it. Add `CONTRIBUTING.md` (what contributions are
   wanted, the gates a PR must pass, the Devnet-only rule), `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`,
   `SECURITY.md` saying plainly that this is a research artifact and not production
   software, and issue and PR templates. ⚠ Added 2026-10-18: the README's *Upstream*
   section describes the filings by outcome and was five weeks stale when E-316 was
   found; run phase 3's survey again on the day the README is finalised. Its *Before
   opting in* item 2 and `docs/explainer/PROTOCOL-FINDINGS-2026-09-02.md` §6 still
   describe #610 as filed, and XLS-0096 PR #604 fixed it: read §7.2 and §7.4 at
   `58ccd30` before rewriting either.
4. **CI.** GitHub Actions running what does not need the vendored build environment:
   the register gates, `check-emphasis`, `check-tables`, `check-web-readonly`,
   `check-filing`, `bun test facility tools`, `tsc --noEmit`. The signer's `cargo test`
   needs `tools/rebuild-env.sh`, which needs network and Conan; either give CI that
   step or document the suite as local-only. **Do not let CI claim coverage it does
   not run.**
5. **Publish.** A squashed publication commit in the public repository; this one keeps
   the full history. The README must say that public history starts at publication and
   that the register's commit citations resolve only in the private archive.
6. **Attribution.** Apache-2.0 is already in `LICENSE`. Check that the vendored
   `mpt-crypto` patch and every third-party citation name their upstream, and that
   `CLAIMS.md` P-7's credits are correct on every public surface.

**Exit:** the repository is public, a stranger can run the test suite from the README,
and `CONTRIBUTING.md` tells them what a good PR looks like.

---

## Phase 2 — the hosted demo

⚠⚠ **This is not a deployment task with a gate attached. It is a trust-boundary
change, and the project's rule is a full adversarial rotation BEFORE implementation.**
Architecture §6 step 8 is *falsified if* *"the API binds to anything but loopback"*
(`ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md:254`), and `ADR-0003`'s trigger 5 fires when any datum
becomes reachable by a party who could not obtain it before. A public deployment is
both. The same tier shipped a decryption oracle once, written by the session that had
just written the trigger (E-212).

1. **Scope it as its own architecture step** with its own falsifier, and write the ADR
   for the hosted surface. The questions it has to answer:
   - **What does the hosted tier serve that a public node does not?** Two things are
     known: `/api/signer` (signer-local status, including a record count covering
     attempts that never reached the ledger) and the decrypted issuer snapshot
     (E-228, E-239). The first is removed from the hosted build. The second is the
     point of the demo and becomes a deliberately published static artifact over
     throwaway Devnet accounts, with the disclosure beside it.
   - **The ledger endpoint.** B-15's named risk acceptance was written for a local
     operator. A hosted page needs its own wording: a lying source makes the page show
     wrong numbers, and nothing signs, so no amount can be published by it.
   - **Devnet resets.** The demo accounts are throwaway. When they vanish the page must
     say so rather than fall back to another source, which is the substitution
     `CLAUDE.md` forbids.
   - Abuse, rate limiting and caching, since the endpoint is no longer one operator's.
2. **The rotation.** Three legs, differed packets, before implementation.
3. **Build.** A hosted build target that excludes the socket client entirely; CORS for
   the deployed origin, which today is a localhost regex; the static issuer snapshot;
   a page that tells a visitor how to run the signer locally to transact.
4. **Extend the gate.** `tools/check-web-readonly.ts` asserts the local shape. The
   hosted surface needs its own part: zero socket clients in the deployed bundle,
   no decryption at request time, and the deployed origin checked against what the
   ADR allows. Verify it by breaking it.
5. **Deploy to Vercel and check the live surface** against the ADR's list, from a
   machine that has never run the project.

**Exit:** a live URL that serves public ledger data and one published snapshot,
carries its disclosures on the page, and cannot sign.

---

## Phase 3 — the two posts

**Gate:** the repository is public and the demo is live, so both posts link to
something a reader can check. Each draft runs `claims-check` and one adversarial read
before it is published, and the owner signs off the final text.

⚠ **Gate amended 2026-10-18, on E-316: every filing is re-surveyed at source before
a word of either post is drafted.** The survey is the one E-316 records: the comments
on each issue this project filed, by anyone but this project; `gh pr view` on every
PR a closing comment names; the fix grepped in the merged text rather than taken from
the comment. Each filing is then described by its outcome on the survey date, not by
its register row. On 2026-10-18 the register carried four filings as open that
upstream had fixed and closed in September; a post written from it would have called
four fixed defects open. Phase 1's README states the same outcomes in its *Upstream*
section and runs the same survey before publication.

1. **The blog post** (`nandan.me/writing`). The honest spine: what XLS-96 hides
   (amounts and balances), what it does not (who, when, how often — P-4, P-11), that
   the issuer can always decrypt (P-9), that there is no rotation or recovery (P-10),
   Devnet only with a dated amendment reading (P-3). Then the part that is genuinely
   this project's own: execution over reading, the adversarial rotations and what they
   caught, the gates that catch a false sentence, and the findings filed upstream.
   Credit Cenk, Malhotra, Akinyele, Chen, Xie, Qian and Dore where the work is theirs
   (P-7). **Do not describe the Trail of Bits report as *the* audit of this code**,
   and do not call a finding *unreported* or *new*: B-30 records a second audit,
   the Sherlock XRP Ledger Attackathon, whose findings this project cannot read,
   and B-31 a third, Zellic's scan of mpt-crypto, known only through the three
   identifiers mpt-crypto#147 cites (added 2026-10-18). Name all three, and say
   which one could be checked.
2. **The LinkedIn post.** Short, answers the question in the first line, links the
   demo, the repository and the post. Same prohibitions. No benchmark number without
   hardware and method (P-8). Nothing that reads as *audited*, *production-ready* or
   *secure* (P-5).

**Exit:** both published, both pointing at a live demo and a public repository.

---

- **The license terms, the public repository's name and owner**, and whether the
  vocabulary translation extends into `docs/reviews/`.
- **Whether CI can run the signer's tests**, which need the vendored build environment.
- **Issue #10**, the unpublished keygen derivation. Publishing it is a new public
  cryptographic artifact and needs its own rotation. It is not a launch blocker, and
  the README should say the derivation is repository-specific and unpublished.
- **The `Observed` rows.** They stay withheld and out of the public tree, by
  `REVIEW-039b` and `REVIEW-039c`.

    48	## The decisions
    49	
    50	### D1. A third review round on the secret sweep
    51	
    52	**Context.** `REVIEW-056d` set a stopping condition before round two ran: no third
    53	round unless round two found a fix that did not discharge its finding. It found
    54	three. The precedent for that case, `REVIEW-049` and E-311, gave the third round
    55	to you with a recommendation.
    56	
    57	**Ruled A, and DONE 2026-10-18 (`REVIEW-056e`).** Round three found 9 findings, no
    58	HIGH; 8 upheld and 1 upheld in part, all fixed; the rotation is closed and its fixes
    59	are unreviewed, as ruled.
    60	
    61	| option | cost | what it buys |
    62	|---|---|---|
    63	| **A. One more leg, on the round-two fixes only, and close after it whatever it finds** *(recommended; ruled)* | about 45 minutes of a Codex leg and an hour of adjudication | the gate that decides publication has had its latest fixes seen |
    64	| B. Close at two | nothing | round two found no HIGH, and its findings moved from coverage to the record's sentences: the convergence pattern `CLAUDE.md` describes |
    65	
    66	### D2. The revision log in a squashed repository
    67	
    68	**Context, measured.** `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS.md` is derived from git history, and
    69	`make check-claims-revisions` fails it if a commit it names is not an ancestor.
    70	In the rehearsal repository it failed: *"db2fd12 is not an ancestor of HEAD"*.
    71	`make ci` runs that gate, so **the public repository's first CI run would fail.**
    72	
    73	| option | cost | what it buys |
    74	|---|---|---|
    75	| **A. Keep the archive's log as a dated, static file, and regenerate a fresh log from the public history** *(recommended; ruled)* | ⚠ priced here as *"a rename and one regeneration"*, and that was wrong: the builder refused to run in a squashed export, because its oracle checks three curated commits that only the private history has. **Built after the ruling**: the oracle skips only when none of its commits exists and `docs/CLAIMS-REVISIONS-ARCHIVE.md` is present. Rehearsed, and `make ci` then passed every step in the export | the per-row dating survives as a record, and the gate keeps protecting the register going forward |
    76	| B. Keep the archive's log and drop the gate from public CI | one line in `tools/ci.sh` | the record survives; nothing in public fails when a register row disappears |
    77	| C. Publish the history instead of squashing | reverses the E-309 ruling, and history carries 1,045 personal-path hits that `--strict-history` fails, so it needs a history rewrite first | the commit citations in the register resolve |
    78	
    79	### D3. The licence, and the vendored `mpt-crypto` patch
    80	
    81	**Context, searched on 2026-10-18 and re-measured that evening.** This repository is
    82	Apache-2.0. It publishes `tools/spike-2026-08-18/wconf-nonces.patch`, 1,657 lines
    83	against `XRPLF/mpt-crypto` at `85dbac7`, across six files under `src/`.
    84	
    85	⚠ **This said *"with 341 context lines of upstream source"*, and that undercounted
    86	it.** The patch's 491 removed lines are upstream source too, so it carries about
    87	832 upstream lines verbatim. ⚠ **A second file was not priced at all.**
    88	`tools/mpt-crypto-bench/wconf_bench.c` says its setup code is *"lifted from the
    89	corresponding upstream test"*, and 70 of its 217 substantive lines appear verbatim
    90	in upstream's `tests/` at the pin. Some of those 70 are API boilerplate. The
    91	search covered the patch and every tracked `.c` and `.h` file, and nothing else.
    92	
    93	**That C repository carries no licence that these searches found:** `gh api repos/XRPLF/mpt-crypto/license`
    94	returns 404, the root listing has no licence file at the pin or at `main`,
    95	`conanfile.py` declares none, and no file under `src/` or `include/` has an SPDX
    96	line, a licence text or a copyright line. No issue or pull request in it mentions
    97	a licence; a control query in the same search returned hits, so the empty result
    98	is real.
    99	
   100	⚠ **The npm package's ISC licence is not evidence about the C source.**
   101	`@xrplf/mpt-crypto@0.1.0` names `XRPLF/xrpl.js` as its repository, and ships
   102	TypeScript and a WASM build of `mpt-crypto` 1.0.4 with no C source. Its `LICENSE`
   103	is an ISC notice with the line *"Copyright (c) 2015 Ripple Labs Inc."*. Option A as
   104	first written cited it as covering the C code, which is a substituted source.
   105	
   106	⚠ **Nothing in the tree carves the upstream lines out of Apache-2.0.** `LICENSE`
   107	is the only licence file and there is no `NOTICE`, so the publication as built
   108	would appear to license Ripple's code under Apache-2.0.
   109	
   110	| option | cost | what it buys |
   111	|---|---|---|
   112	| **A′. Publish the patch and the harness, with a `NOTICE` that carves them out** *(recommended; ruled)* | one `NOTICE` file and one README line, committed before the export | a stranger can rebuild the signer, and the tree stops appearing to relicense upstream code. ⚠ The redistribution itself stays unlicensed, and is accepted by name |
   113	| A. Publish the patch, citing the npm package's ISC licence | none now | ⚠ **not recommended**: it rests on a grant nothing states |
   114	| B. Ask upstream to add a licence file first, then A | an upstream filing and its wait | **ruled out by the owner, 2026-10-18** |
   115	| C. Publish without the patch or the lifted harness | the signer becomes buildable only from the private archive | no upstream code is republished; **the fallback if the copyright holder objects** |
   116	| D. Move the patch to a GitHub fork of `mpt-crypto` | rework `tools/rebuild-env.sh` and `make vendor-verify`, and rebuild the signer, just before publication | the copy rests on GitHub's forking grant. ⚠ It does not settle modification: *"Although a code host such as GitHub may allow you to view and fork the code, this does not imply that you are permitted to use, modify, or share the software"* (choosealicense.com, read 2026-10-18) |
   117	
   118	**Why A′ over C.** The signer is the main thing this project built, and its point
   119	is that a claim is checked by running it. Under C a stranger cannot build it. The
   120	practical risk under A′ is low: the code is published openly by the organisation
   121	whose surrounding stack is ISC, and this project's use of it sends findings back
   122	upstream. A′ cannot make the copy licensed. What it does is stop this repository
   123	from mislabelling someone else's code, which is the part of the risk this project
   124	controls.
   125	
   126	**The `NOTICE`, drafted** on the pattern `evm-confidential-positions` already uses:
   127	
   128	```text
   129	xrpl-confidential-positions
   130	Copyright 2026 Nandan
   131	
   132	Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (see LICENSE).
   133	
   134	This repository includes material from a third party that is NOT covered by
   135	that licence:
   136	
   137	- tools/spike-2026-08-18/wconf-nonces.patch is a patch against XRPLF/mpt-crypto
   138	  at 85dbac7. Its context lines and removed lines, about 832, are upstream source.
   139	- tools/mpt-crypto-bench/wconf_bench.c lifts proof setup code from mpt-crypto's
   140	  tests at the same commit.
   141	
   142	XRPLF/mpt-crypto carried no licence file at that commit or at main when checked
   143	on 2026-10-18. The npm package @xrplf/mpt-crypto is ISC-licensed; it ships a WASM
   144	build and no C source, and this repository does not treat its licence as a grant
   145	for the C source. The upstream lines are not relicensed here. They are
   146	reproduced so that the signer can be rebuilt, and they will be removed if the
   147	copyright holder asks.
   148	```
   149	
   150	Plan item 6, attribution, also asks that every third-party citation name its
   151	upstream and that P-7's credits be right on every public surface. **That check
   152	was not run this session**, beyond the README's credit section, which now names
   153	all three audits.
   154	
   155	### D4. The public repository's name and owner
   156	
   157	**Recommended and ruled: the public repository is `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions`,
   158	and the private one is renamed `xrpl-confidential-positions-private` first.** That
   159	is the pattern the EVM study already uses. `nandanito/evm-confidential-positions-private`
   160	is private and `nandanito/evm-confidential-positions` is public, created the next
   161	day (both read with `gh repo view` on 2026-10-18). The private repository holds the
   162	name today, so the name has to be freed before the public one can take it. The
   163	personal account matches the blog and the LinkedIn post. An organisation adds
   164	nothing for a single-maintainer research artifact.
   165	
   166	**The rename, read on GitHub's rename page on 2026-10-18.** After a rename, *"all
   167	existing information, with the exception of project site URLs, is automatically
   168	redirected to the new name."* If a new repository then takes the old name,
   169	*"redirects to the renamed repository will no longer work."* Here that is what is
   170	wanted: the canonical name should land on the public repository. Every existing
   171	clone needs `git remote set-url origin` to the new name. The page also says
   172	*"GitHub will not redirect calls to an action hosted by a renamed repository"*;
   173	this repository hosts no action.
   174	
   175	**Ruled, with one addition: the local folder keeps its name.** This checkout stays
   176	at `…/xrpl-confidential-positions`. Claude Code's memory is keyed by the folder's
   177	path, and the owner found that renaming the EVM study's folder broke both its Claude
   178	Code and its Codex sessions. Only its `origin` changes, to `xrpl-confidential-positions-private`. The
   179	public repository's working copy is a separate folder, the step 4 export.
   180	
   181	⚠ **That leaves a folder whose name matches the PUBLIC repository holding the
   182	PRIVATE history**, and one mistake publishes all of it: the history that
   183	`--strict-history` fails on 1,045 personal-path hits, and the 36 commits behind the
   184	withheld rows. Once the public repository takes the old name, a stale `origin` in
   185	any clone stops redirecting to the archive and points at the public repository
   186	instead. **So step 8 also installs a `pre-push` hook in every private clone that
   187	refuses any remote URL naming `nandanito/xrpl-confidential-positions` without the
   188	`-private` suffix**, and the public URL is never added as a remote here.
   189	
   190	The README's footnote records `xrpl-confidential-positions` as held since
   191	2026-08-16, which stays true. The five-minute path says *"from a fresh clone"* and
   192	names no URL, so nothing else changes.
   193	
   194	### D5. Whether the vocabulary translation reaches the records
   195	
   196	| option | cost | what it buys |
   197	|---|---|---|
   198	| **A. Leave `docs/reviews/`, `docs/decisions/` and `CLAIMS.md` as written, under the banner** *(recommended, and what is built)* | none | the records keep their value as a record of what was believed |
   199	| B. Translate them in the published tree only | a scripted pass at publication | a public reader never meets the shorthand; the published records then differ from the archive's |
   200	
   201	### D6. Whether CI runs the signer's tests
   202	
   203	**Context.** The signer links a patched `mpt-crypto` that `tools/rebuild-env.sh`
   204	builds with Homebrew `openssl@3`, a macOS build. `make ci` therefore skips
   205	`cargo test`, `check-test-support` and `check-zeroize-bounds`, says so on every
   206	run, and reports five signer-starting facility tests as skipped rather than passed.
   207	
   208	| option | cost | what it buys |
   209	|---|---|---|
   210	| **A. Local-only, as built** *(recommended for publication)* | none | an honest CI that names what it does not run |
   211	| B. A macOS runner job: `tools/rebuild-env.sh` then `cargo test` | unverified; pinned GitHub clones at every run; its own verification round | the signer is tested on every pull request |
   212	| C. Port the vendored build to Linux | rework of `build.sh` and `rebuild-env.sh` | the same, on the cheaper runner |
   213	
   214	### D7. Copying the issues, so the numbers the documents cite resolve
   215	
   216	**Context, measured on 2026-10-18.** The README says bare issue numbers are issues
   217	on *this* repository. `git grep` finds the pattern `issue #N` in 119 files, most of
   218	them under `docs/reviews/` and `docs/decisions/`. Every number from #1 to #11
   219	appears, and #13; #1 appears 157 times, #10 70 times and #11 50 times. That counts
   220	the pattern, and a few hits are upstream issues rather than this repository's.
   221	These numbers belong to the private repository, which has 13 issues, three of them open (#5, #8, #10), 17
   222	comments between them and no pull requests. A fresh public repository numbers from
   223	#1, and issues and pull requests share one counter, so every citation would point
   224	at the wrong issue or at nothing.
   225	
   226	⚠ **Six of the 13 cite withheld rows, and some restate them.** The bodies of #6
   227	(U-28), #7 (U-31) and #10 (U-32), and comments on #2 (U-36), #3 (U-37) and #4
   228	(U-35, U-38), cite rows that are in `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md` and not
   229	in `UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md`. #10's body also restates what U-32 says. **Copying them
   230	verbatim would publish part of what E-240 withheld.**
   231	
   232	| option | cost | what it buys |
   233	|---|---|---|
   234	| **A. Title-only stubs for the ten closed issues, and full copies of the three open ones** *(recommended; ruled)* | 13 issues created in order, with #10's U-32 passage redacted and every body run through the secret sweep first | every cited number resolves, and no withheld row id leaves, because #10 is the only one of the open three that cites one. ⚠ The scan matched row ids, so the three open bodies still need a read for restated content. The closed issues' outcomes are already in the published register and reviews |
   235	| B. Full copies of all 13 with their comments, after a redaction pass you approve | six issues redacted by hand and 17 comments copied | the discussions themselves become public |
   236	| C. No copies. The step 6 sentence also says issue numbers refer to the archive, and the open three are re-filed under new numbers | the citations to #8 and #10 edited | ⚠ the public repository's own #1 to #13 would then collide with every cited number |
   237	
   238	A stub carries the original title and says: *"A stub. This was issue #N in the
   239	private archive, opened DATE and closed DATE. Its discussion stays there; where
   240	this repository cites issue #N, the outcome is in the document that cites it."*
   241	⚠ **The issues have to be created before anything else can take a number**, so
   242	the public repository is created private and made public only after them.
   243

   251	   (`REVIEW-056e`).
   252	4. Export the tree with `git archive` into a fresh repository and commit it as
   253	   one commit. Confirm with `git ls-files` that `docs/upstream-observed-WITHHELD.md`
   254	   and `docs/evidence/f12-emsdk-2026-10-18/logs-unscrubbed/` are absent, and that
   255	   `NOTICE` is present.

   257	   commit, then run `bun run tools/build-claims-revisions.ts` and commit the
   258	   regenerated log. D5 needs nothing: the records keep their wording.

   260	   publication. This repository was developed privately from 2026-08-16 and

   262	   `docs/reviews/` refer to that private archive and do not resolve here."* Under
   263	   D7's option A, add: *"Issues #1 to #13 were copied from the archive in order, so
   264	   the issue numbers cited here resolve; the ten closed ones are stubs."*
   265	7. **Run the phase 1 gate on that exact commit**, with the local store:

   267	8. **Apply D4**: rename the private repository to `xrpl-confidential-positions-private`,
   268	   run `git remote set-url origin` in every clone, and confirm with `git ls-remote origin`.
   269	   Install the `pre-push` guard in every private clone, and prove it refuses by
   270	   pushing to the public URL before that repository exists.

   272	   push the one commit. **Apply D7 before anything else can take a number**: put
   273	   the 13 bodies in a scratch repository and run the sweep over it, create the

   275	   match. Then make it public, and set: private vulnerability reporting on, which
   276	   `SECURITY.md` and `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md` rely on; branch protection requiring the
   277	   `ci` check; and, recommended, the workflow's actions pinned by commit SHA rather
   278	   than tag.
   279	10. **Watch the first CI run.** The workflow has never run on GitHub; `tools/ci.sh`
   280	    has run only on macOS, in this checkout and in a fresh clone.

75:| E-34 | **Nonce reuse in a compact-form sigma proof leaks `sk_A`, and is undetectable from the proofs.** Compact form transmits `(e, z…)` not the commitments, so reused nonces yield proofs that verify normally while allowing an observer holding both to extract the witness. ⚠ **That mitigation line is FALSE and was falsified by two independent cryptography reviewers on 2026-08-17 (REVIEW-005a, REVIEW-005b, adjudicated in REVIEW-005c §1).** `statement` does not include `TransactionContextID` as the paper defines it, while the Fiat–Shamir challenge does — so an ordinary resubmission at a changed sequence or `CB_S_Version`, with cached ciphertexts, yields two proofs sharing first-round commitments under distinct challenges. That is Theorem 4.2's extraction condition exactly. **The formula as written causes the failure it is quoted to prevent.** The correct requirement is that the nonce preimage must refine the challenge preimage — every byte that can influence the challenge, other than the commitments, must appear injectively in the derivation. See F-17 | `cmpt-compact-sigma.pdf` §6.1, Remark 6.1. **Direct input to ADR-0001 §(e) and the Rust signer — ranks above TOB-RIPCTX-5, since it needs only a coding error, not an adversary with local measurement** | A |

The library it pins, mpt-crypto `1.0.2`, still refuses a Send unless each participant's first 33 bytes equal the first participant's, so the extra copies must be identical and carry nothing. The paper's own components at those enforced sizes sum to 1276 B with an auditor and 1210 B without, which are the sizes F-13 measured: replacing the paper's one shared `C₁` plus n `C₂` with n full 66-byte pairs adds 99 B at n=4, and at n=3 the paper's total is first 33 B lower and 66 B is then added. The generator prints the current distribution with its predicate.** The project owner asked for a topic-based split into separate files. ⚠⚠ **THE NUMBERS THIS ROW FIRST GAVE CAME FROM AN INSTRUMENT THAT WAS THEN DISCARDED, AND `REVIEW-040` H3 IS THE CORRECTION.** It said *"93 of 245 in four or more, only 49 (20%) in exactly one"* — measured with an **exploratory nine-topic pass over WHOLE ROWS**, run before the classifier existed. **What ships is twelve topics matched against HEADLINES.** ⚠⚠ **AND THE CORRECTION WAS ITSELF WRONG — `REVIEW-041`, THIRD FIGURE FOR ONE MEASUREMENT.** *"113 in exactly one topic (46.1%), 4 in four or more"* counted **rule matches only**; a leg counted **final assignment** and got 121, another got 119, and **all three are right for different predicates** — rules-only vs rules+overrides, which cohort, which commit. ⚠ **A bare number here was meaningless without its predicate**, which is E-236 landing for the fourth time. **No figure is quoted now: `bun run tools/build-claims-topics.ts` PRINTS the distribution with its predicate stated**, derived where it is computed. ⚠ **The figure that justified the design decision was not reproducible from the tree**, which is this project's own standing prohibition, and an external leg reproduced neither. **The 80%-in-four-or-more figure is real only for WHOLE-ROW matching (197/245, 80.4%), which is not what the classifier reads.** ⚠ **A leg's own recount gave 121 (49.4%) where this gives 113 (46.1%); the disagreement is recorded and unresolved.** **What survives: roughly HALF the rows are multi-topic, so a split still forces a single home on about 120 of them** — a materially weaker argument than the one first written. E-203 is a gate that false-passed *and* a miscount *and* a review finding *and* a lesson about stripping string literals. ⚠ **A PHYSICAL SPLIT FILES SUCH A ROW UNDER ONE TOPIC AND HIDES IT FROM READERS OF THE OTHERS.** ⚠⚠ **AND `REVIEW-040` H3 UPHELD THAT THIS WAS PUT AS A FALSE DICHOTOMY: a physical split and generated secondary indexes are NOT mutually exclusive** — rows could live in one primary file and still be surfaced under every tag. The choice made here is defensible; it was argued as if it were forced, and it was not. **Three further costs, each measured on the register: 63% of rows cite another row (476 cross-references in total), which a move scatters; the chronology is load-bearing, because *"seventh instance of a wrong count"* is only legible in an append-only file in order; and every gate that opens `CLAIMS.md` by path would need updating and re-verifying by breaking it.** ⚠ **The classification instrument is the row's HEADLINE, not the row.** Matching whole rows put **80% in 4+ topics**, because an evidence cell mentions the gate, the review, the Devnet run and the secret alike; the headline states what the row is *about* | **`tools/build-claims-topics.ts`, `make check-claims-topics`, `docs/claims/by-topic/` — 12 topic files plus a README, every parsed row carrying at least one topic.** ⚠ **This said *"317 rows"* and the tree said 318 — the count was true when written and false once E-245 itself was added, which is E-236's self-referential shape inside the row documenting a generator built to stop restated numbers (`REVIEW-040` M3). No row count is stated here now; the gate prints it.** The rows are **not moved**: `CLAIMS.md` stays canonical and chronological, and a row appears under **every** topic it carries, which is the property a split cannot have. ⚠ **COMPLETENESS IS THE GATE'S PROPERTY, not byte-comparison** — it FAILS when a row matches no topic, when an override names a row the register no longer contains or a topic that no longer exists, and when a topic file is **orphaned** by a removed topic. ⚠ **VERIFIED BY BREAKING IT IN FIVE DIRECTIONS, each exiting non-zero, with a negative control on the clean tree that must pass**: a hand-edited file, an orphaned file, an override naming `E-9999`, an override naming an unknown topic, and a row left carrying no topic. ⚠⚠ **AND THE FIRST RUN FOUND A DEFECT IN THE IMPORT ITSELF**: `build-claims-topics.ts` imports `summarise` from `build-claims-index.ts`, whose main body was **top-level**, so merely importing it **rewrote `docs/CLAIMS-INDEX.md`** — and under `--check` would have run *that* gate's exit path from inside another tool. **Fixed with `if (import.meta.main) main()`, and the index gate was re-verified by breaking it afterwards**, because modifying a gated tool voids its previous verification. ⚠ **LEXICAL, over a NAMED SET** of headline patterns plus an override list; a topic is a finding aid and says nothing about whether a row is true. ⚠ **`REVIEW-040` M8: *"eleven of the first thirty-seven"* overrides and the E-87/E-90 comment-out both happened and NEITHER is recoverable from a commit** — no 37-entry tree was preserved. They are retrospective prose and are marked as such. ⚠⚠ **TWO MORE DEFECTS, BOTH FOUND BY THE GATE ON THE TREE THAT BUILT IT.** (1) **Eleven of the first thirty-seven overrides were REDUNDANT within the hour** — patterns widened after the list was written, so eleven entries each asserted *the rules do not reach this row* when they did. **A redundant override is a false statement about the rules and nothing would have said so**, which is the hand-maintained-list rot this register has recorded repeatedly; the gate now FAILS on one and the fix is deleting the line. (2) **Pruning those eleven mechanically COMMENTED OUT TWO LIVE ENTRIES** — the list was packed several to a line, so deleting one pulled `E-87` and `E-90` up onto a `//` comment. ⚠ **Caught only because the completeness check then reported both as carrying no topic**; a byte-comparison gate would have regenerated happily and dropped two rows from two topics. **The list is now one entry per line, with the reason at the top.** ⚠ **Verified by breaking it in SIX directions with a negative control that must pass** | A, measured |
306:| E-246 | ⚠⚠ **`REVIEW-040` — FOUR LEGS ON THE BRANCH THAT FIXED E-243, AND THE WORST FINDING IS THAT THE JUSTIFICATION FOR ITS MAIN DESIGN DECISION CAME FROM AN INSTRUMENT THAT WAS THEN DISCARDED (H3, see E-245).** Three externals with deliberately differed packets — a detector attack with no repository, an evidence audit with read-only access at the frozen commit, and **a READER asked only whether they could FIND things** — plus a self leg written to a scratchpad and committed after the others returned. ⚠ **THE OTHER TWO HIGHs ARE BOTH IN THE GATE THIS BRANCH EXTENDED.** (1) **A header whose cell count differs from its delimiter renders NO TABLE AT ALL in GFM, and both of the gate's properties passed it** — `scan()` takes its width from the separator and never looks at the header. **Found independently by two legs on two models.** ⚠ **Third distinct way this gate has certified a file whose rows do not render: a blank line (E-243), prose and `---` (E-244), and this.** (2) **A claim row written `\|E-9999\| … \|`, with no space after the leading pipe, is valid GFM and BOTH GENERATORS refused to parse it** — absent from the index and every topic view, with all gates green. ⚠⚠ **That bounds the completeness property this branch was proudest of: *every row carries a topic* is quantified over PARSED rows, and an unparsed row is invisible rather than incomplete.** ⚠ **And three of the eight grades E-244 assigned are the WRONG KIND** — E-169, E-172 and E-174 read `A, executed` where their own evidence cells support *source* or *measured*; **E-174 most sharply, because its `cargo build -vv` evidence sits in the CLAIM cell, not the Evidence cell.** The self leg caught one of the three, which is what a session auditing its own judgement is worth | **Fixed, and the fix round found a defect NEITHER round one nor any leg had reported.** ⚠⚠ **VERIFYING THE PIPE-ESCAPE FIX EXPOSED THAT BOTH GENERATORS SPLIT ROWS WITH `line.split("\|")`, WHICH IS WRONG FOR EVERY ROW CARRYING A PIPE INSIDE A CODE SPAN** — the claim cell is cut at the pipe and the wrong cell is then read as the grade. **Seven live rows disagree with their true cell count, and `E-48` and `B-21` were printing a FRAGMENT OF PROSE where their grade belongs, in the shipped index.** ⚠ **`check-table-integrity` had this right all along — it strips code spans before counting — so the GATE was correct while the two tools reading the same rows were not.** ⚠ **`REVIEW-042` NOTE: the splitter described next was corrected twice more** — its code-span awareness was the WRONG DIRECTION (`REVIEW-041`) and its escaped-backslash handling disagreed with `cmark-gfm` (`REVIEW-042`). **Only `\|` is content.** A shared `splitCells()` splits on unescaped pipes, is used by both generators, and is **probed on six shapes before either runs**; disabling its code-span branch exits non-zero. **The gate now checks header/delimiter width** (H1), **both `ROW` regexes tolerate the missing space** (H2), **`summarise()` escapes pipes rather than emitting them structurally**, and ⚠ **the 13 generated files are now IN `TARGETS`** — they were not, so nothing shape-checked the output of a generator that could emit a broken row. ⚠ **Scanned files went 2 → 16.** ⚠⚠ **The row first said *"cell-checked rows 357 → 1231"* and `REVIEW-041` showed that SPLICED TWO TREES**: 1231 is the parent commit's corpus measured against 16 targets, while the fixed tree reports **1241**. **A before/after pair must come from one tree each, and these did not.** **The redundancy check is now PER MEMBER, not per entry** (M4), and on its first run it caught **three** overrides the rules already covered, including the `E-111` instance a leg named. **`headline()` no longer trusts a bold lead under 40 characters** (M5) — `E-17`'s headline was `"When set"`, and the override that hid it is deleted. `isSeparator` accepts a single-hyphen delimiter (L1), `stripCode` was changed twice more after this — see E-247 and `REVIEW-042`. **Controls across two helpers, each counted by the helper that runs it** — ⚠ **no total is written here, because the L1 fix moved it and E-244's *"nine controls"* went stale the same day.** ⚠ **Two legs reported right diagnoses with wrong numbers and both are recorded as rejected-with-reasons** — and **the first re-measurement of one of them was also wrong**, settled only by running a real markdown parser instead of reasoning about GFM | A, measured |
307:| E-247 | ⚠⚠ **`REVIEW-041` — ROUND TWO, TWO EXTERNAL LEGS, AND THE HEADLINE IS A FALSE PREMISE THE GATE WAS BUILT ON. IN A GFM TABLE A PIPE IS A CELL DELIMITER EVEN INSIDE A CODE SPAN.** The spec requires escaping a pipe to place it in a cell, *"including inside other inline spans"*. ⚠ **`stripCode()` protected code-span pipes and the gate's own header ARGUED FOR IT** — *"a naive `count('\|')` flags every one of them and the gate becomes noise that gets switched off"*. **That rationale was false: those rows were not noise, they were broken, and the gate was built to not see them.** ⚠⚠ **FIVE LIVE ROWS RENDERED WITH MORE CELLS THAN THEIR HEADER, AND GFM DISCARDS THE EXCESS, SO THE GRADE COLUMN DISPLAYED THE WRONG THING** — `E-162` showed *"Finding"*, `E-222` showed *"sort -u"*, `E-214` showed a fragment of code. ⚠⚠ **AND ONE OF THE FIVE WAS `E-246`, THE ROW WRITTEN THAT DAY TO RECORD ROUND ONE: the register entry documenting a rendering defect was itself one.** ⚠ **Fourth distinct way this gate has certified a file whose rows do not render** — a blank line (E-243), prose and `---` (E-244), a header/delimiter mismatch (`REVIEW-040` H1), and this. **Found independently by the self leg and by the Codex leg, which corroborated with a second parser** | **19 pipes escaped across the five rows; `stripCode` and `splitCells` now implement GFM semantics — only `\|` is content — and `check-table-integrity` counts the same way both generators split.** ⚠ **The self-test that asserted the falsehood is INVERTED and carries its own history**, and the header rationale is retracted in place rather than deleted. **Verified by breaking it against the real tree**: unescaping `E-162`'s pipes again exits 1. ⚠⚠ **ROUND TWO'S FIRST FIX WENT THE WRONG WAY** — `splitCells` was given code-span awareness to stop the generators truncating claim cells, which moved them AWAY from the renderer; **the register was the thing that was wrong.** ⚠⚠ **AND THE `~20%` FIGURE WAS CORRECTED TO A THIRD WRONG NUMBER.** Round one's replacement, *"113 (46.1%)"*, counted rule matches only; one leg counted final assignment and got 121, another 119, and **all three reproduce exactly under their own predicate** — the defect was the UNSTATED PREDICATE, E-236 a fourth time. **No figure is quoted now: the generator prints the distribution with its predicate named.** ⚠ **The discarded numbers were live in FOUR places and round one fixed one** — `E-245`'s own headline, the `Makefile`, the generator docstring and the handover all still carried them, which is E-168's shape inside the correction for it. ⚠ **Two more count splices found and fixed**: *"357 → 1231"* took its two halves from two different trees (the fixed tree reports 1241), and *"nine controls"* went stale the same day the L1 fix made it ten. ⚠ **CONVERGENCE NOT REACHED and the condition is stated in `REVIEW-041d`**: round two's headline is a CONSTRUCTION defect, not an instrument one, so the two-round stopping condition did not fire | A, measured |
308:| E-248 | ⚠⚠ **`REVIEW-042` — ROUND THREE, RUN AGAINST GITHUB'S OWN PARSER, AND IT FOUND THAT ROUND TWO'S GFM FIX WAS ITSELF WRONG ABOUT ESCAPES.** `REVIEW-041d` scoped this round in advance: *whether the escaped rows and the GFM-semantics gate hold against a parser this project has not used*. **`cmark-gfm 0.29.0.gfm.13` — the reference implementation GitHub renders with — and `markdown-it` both report 17 files, 1256 body rows, ZERO wrong-width and ZERO stray.** ⚠ **THE HIGH: a `\|` preceded by ANOTHER backslash.** Round two parked `\\` before stripping, so a doubled backslash made the following pipe structural. **`cmark-gfm` and Pandoc both disagree — GFM's table scanner does a ONE-CHARACTER LOOKBACK and no escape-counting, so that pipe is content.** ⚠ **The gate would have passed a row whose Grade the renderer moves into Evidence — the exact shape round two existed to eliminate, reintroduced by round two's own fix.** Latent; no such row exists. ⚠⚠ **AND THE DOCSTRING ABOVE `stripCode` STILL READ *"Remove inline code spans, so pipes inside backticks are not cells"*** — the sentence `REVIEW-041` retracted, surviving one more round above the corrected body, **E-168's shape inside the fix for a finding whose lesson is E-168** | **`stripCode` is now one `replace(/\\\|/g, "")` and `splitCells` does the one-character lookback, VALIDATED CELL-FOR-CELL AGAINST `cmark-gfm` OVER TWELVE ROW SHAPES** — plain, escaped pipe, doubled backslash, single and double code spans, escaped pipe inside a span, no trailing pipe, empty cell, inline HTML, a lone backslash pair. **Eleven match exactly; the twelfth differs only because the comparison strips HTML tags, and its boundaries agree.** ⚠⚠ **THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS ROUND'S OWN CHECK COULD NOT FAIL**: comparing `<td>` count to `<th>` count always passes, because GFM pads and truncates every row to the header width — it reported *clean* on `cb72368`, where four rows were known broken. **The working instrument compares the author's last cell to the rendered one**, and gives 4 discarded at `cb72368`, **5 at `1733bfd` — round one's own `E-246` joined them** — and **0 now.** ⚠ **Two more sweep hits were false positives OF THE SWEEP** (markdown links), caught by checking the instrument before believing it. **Also fixed: `isRow` accepted any indentation and demanded a trailing pipe, while the generators capped at three spaces and did not** — GFM allows at most three and the trailing pipe is optional, so the gate and the tools reading the same file did not recognise the same language. **The distribution gloss pointed at the SINGLETON share when the forced-choice population is the MULTI-topic rows**, and the handover's largest-topic count was stale. ⚠⚠ **CONVERGENCE REACHED and the reasoning is in `REVIEW-042d`: round three found NO live construction defect** — every finding is an instrument, a docstring, a regex boundary with no file that hits it, or a gloss. **Three rounds, nine legs, three parsers.** A fourth round would review the fixes to a docstring | A, measured |
309:| E-249 | **`CLAIMS.md`'s `Revision history` stopped being maintained on 2026-08-25 and nobody noticed for fifteen days.** It was last given content at `f9e9eb9`, when the register held 203 rows; at the rewrite it held 321, with 62 commits touching the file in between and not one of them recorded. ⚠ The cause is the shape rather than neglect: the log was a single paragraph of 4,811 characters, so the surface that would have shown a session it was stale is the surface nobody read. <div align="center">

# W-CONF

### A Confidential MPT reference application for the XRP Ledger.

*Built to find out what XLS-96 actually hides — by execution, not by reading.*

[![License](https://img.shields.io/badge/license-Apache--2.0-3b82f6)](LICENSE)
[![Network](https://img.shields.io/badge/network-Devnet%20only-e11d48)](#amendment-status)
[![Spec](https://img.shields.io/badge/XLS--0096-Final-22c55e)](https://github.com/XRPLF/XRPL-Standards/tree/master/XLS-0096-confidential-mpt)
[![Signer](https://img.shields.io/badge/signer-Rust-000000?logo=rust&logoColor=white)](signer/)
[![Runtime](https://img.shields.io/badge/runtime-Bun-000000?logo=bun&logoColor=white)](https://bun.sh)
[![Status](https://img.shields.io/badge/status-research%20artifact-6366f1)](#what-is-open)

[**Trust model**](#trust-model) · [**Run it**](#running-it) · [**Protocol findings**](docs/explainer/PROTOCOL-FINDINGS-2026-09-02.md) · [**What is open**](#what-is-open) · [**Claims register**](CLAIMS.md)

</div>

---

**A confidential receivables facility on the XRP Ledger.** A bank opens a facility. Suppliers enrol, draw down against invoices, and repay. Every position is an encrypted balance on a public ledger, and the facility retires in tranches. It has executed on Devnet, and the point of building it was to establish exactly what an outside observer sees while it happens.

**The answer: it hides the amount of a confidential *transfer*, and balances — and nothing else.** ⚠ **Converting in or out publishes the amount in plaintext, every time**; only a `Send` hides one, so the figure entering confidential form and every figure leaving it are public forever. Who paid whom, when, and how often are public forever; the issuer can decrypt every balance; and one asymmetry matters — a *freshly initialised* balance is publicly recognisable as zero while a *spent-down* one is not. That is the protocol, not a limitation of this build. [**Trust model**](#trust-model) sets it out in full, and it is the section to read before any other.

> ⚠ **Devnet only. No mainnet writes, at any phase, for any reason.**
> Nothing here is audited, production-ready, or safe to put value behind.

**What it exercises:** Multi-Purpose Tokens (XLS-33) and Confidential Transfers (XLS-96), together. ⚠ **Not Credentials (XLS-70) or Permissioned Domains (XLS-80)** — gating an issuance to credentialled accounts needs a `PermissionedDomainSet` and a credential per supplier, which is a separate milestone that has not been built. The issuance carries no `sfDomainID` and the facility says so when it runs.

**It has a second output.** Building carefully against a young specification surfaces things its authors would want to know. XLS-0096 was created on 2026-01-15 and was a Draft for most of this project's life. Upstream marked it `Final` on 2026-09-03, in PR #604, and last changed it on 2026-09-10, when it took in errata this project filed. Both dates were read on 2026-10-18 (E-1, E-316). ⚠ **This paragraph and the badge above called it a Draft until 2026-10-18.** Those accumulate in [`UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md`](UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md) and are filed under [`docs/UPSTREAM-FILING-PROTOCOL.md`](docs/UPSTREAM-FILING-PROTOCOL.md), held to a higher evidence bar than anything published here.

> **Reading the citations.** Claims here carry a row id from [`CLAIMS.md`](CLAIMS.md), the register that governs what this project may say. **`E-`** is established evidence, **`F-`** a finding about the protocol or its implementation, **`P-`** a standing prohibition — something this project may never claim — and **`B-`** an open blocker. `ADR-nnnn` is a decision record and `REVIEW-nnn` a review round, both under [`docs/decisions/`](docs/decisions/) and [`docs/reviews/`](docs/reviews/); bare issue numbers are issues on **this** repository. Where a sentence here disagrees with its register row, the row wins.

<details>
<summary><b>Terms used below, if you are new to XLS-96 or the XRPL</b> — click to expand</summary>

| term | what it means here |
|---|---|
| **MPT** | Multi-Purpose Token (XLS-33). `MPTokenIssuance` is the issuer's object defining one token; an `MPToken` is one holder's position in it. |
| **Confidential balance** | A holder's balance held as an ElGamal ciphertext on the public ledger rather than as a plaintext integer. Two of them per holder: a *spending* balance and an *inbox* that receives transfers. |
| **`sk_A`** | The holder's own ElGamal secret key — the `A` is for the account. It decrypts that holder's balance and is a required input to the proof for a `ConfidentialMPTSend`. |
| **Encrypted zero (`EncZero`)** | The *canonical* ciphertext of zero — fixed and recomputable by anyone from public inputs. ⚠ It is written on first-time initialisation, merge reset and clawback only. A balance spent down to zero is a randomised encryption of zero and is **not** recognisable, so `EncZero` identifies how a balance became zero, not whether it is. |
| **Convert / Send / ConvertBack** | `ConfidentialMPTConvert` moves plaintext into confidential form; `ConfidentialMPTSend` moves a hidden amount between holders; `ConfidentialMPTConvertBack` moves it back out. **Convert and ConvertBack publish their amounts in plaintext**; only Send hides one. |
| **Bulletproof / compact sigma** | The proofs a confidential transaction carries. **The composition differs per transaction type.** `Send`: a 192-byte compact sigma plus a 754-byte aggregated Bulletproof. `ConvertBack`: a 128-byte sigma plus a 688-byte Bulletproof. `Convert`: a 64-byte Schnorr key-possession proof. `Clawback`: a 64-byte issuer-side compact sigma — ⚠ **same size as `Convert`'s, different construct, different party**. `MergeInbox`: **no proof**. The Bulletproof dominates wherever there is one. |
| **`tec` / `ter` codes** | XRPL transaction results. ⚠ **A `tec` is *applied to the ledger*** — it consumes a sequence number and its fields persist — which is why a failed confidential transaction still publishes its ciphertexts, and why `fail_hard` matters. |
| **`fail_hard`** | The JSON submission parameter asking the node to discard a failing transaction rather than apply it. `TapFailHard` is the corresponding internal apply flag in rippled. ⚠ **Internal documents in this repository call this `tapFailHard`, which is this project's shorthand and appears zero times in rippled** — see [`docs/VOCABULARY.md`](docs/VOCABULARY.md). |
| **Family seed / master seed** | The base-58 secret an XRPL account is derived from. This project also derives the holder's ElGamal key from it, which is what makes the seed a permanent decryption credential. |
| **Dedicated Account** | The facility's own holding account. ⚠ **Never call it a vault**: an XLS-65 `Vault` cannot convert to confidential form, and XLS-0096's own unrelated use of *"Confidential Vault"* for this account has already pointed one reading at the wrong conclusion. |
| **UDS** | Unix domain socket. A local IPC channel that no browser API can open, which is the whole reason the signer uses one. |
| **XLS-65 / XLS-66** | The ledger's single-asset vault standard and its lending protocol. Both come up because a confidential balance does not compose with either. |
| **Amendment** | An XRPL protocol feature that must be voted on and enabled per network. Whether one is live is a *reading*, never knowledge. |

</details>

---
