    39	
    40	## Leg B — Gemini (diff only, no file access)
    41	
    42	# Adversarial Code & Research Review
    43	
    44	## Priority Focus Assessment
    45	
    46	### 1. Reimplementation of `canonicalEncZero` in `observer-harness.ts`
    47	- **Buffer layout (51 bytes)**: Exact match for `7 ("EncZero") + 20 (AccountID) + 24 (MPTokenIssuanceID)`. Offsets `0`, `7`, and `27` are correct.
    48	- **Rejection sampling & termination**: `isValidScalar` strictly enforces $0 < n < \text{CURVE.n}$. With secp256k1 order $n \approx 2^{256} - 2^{128}$, rejection probability is $\approx 2^{-128}$, ensuring termination in 1 iteration with overwhelming probability.
    49	- **Scalar parsing**: `BigInt("0x" + hex(r))` correctly handles leading zero bytes as big-endian integers in JavaScript.
    50	- **Compressed encoding**: `@noble/curves` `Point.prototype.toRawBytes(true)` outputs standard 33-byte SEC1 compressed points ($0x02/0x03$ prefix + $X$-coord). Concatenating $C_1$ and $C_2$ produces the exact 66-byte (132 hex characters) ElGamal ciphertext expected on-chain for $m = 0$ ($C_1 = rG, C_2 = rQ$).
    51	- **Defects identified**: Undeclared package dependency on `ripple-address-codec` prevents execution ([Finding 1](#finding-1-high---toolsobserver-harnessts38-packagejson12-18)); missing length check on `issuanceIdHex` can throw unhandled `RangeError` ([Finding 7](#finding-7-low---toolsobserver-harnessts60-65)).
    52	
    53	### 2. `--assert-encrypted-zero` Assertion Soundness
    54	- **Trivial pass risk**: Cannot pass trivially. Matching an on-chain 66-byte ciphertext requires exact byte equality with live ledger state ($2^{-528}$ random match probability). The negative control (`perturbedAccount`) verifies that flipping a bit in the AccountID produces a distinct ciphertext that collides with none of the account's ciphertext fields.
    55	- **False failure risk**: A user passing a lowercase `MPTokenIssuanceID` on the CLI causes the object lookup to fail before assertion evaluation due to case-sensitive matching against rippled's uppercase response ([Finding 2](#finding-2-high---toolsobserver-harnessts128-toolsissuer-decrypt-balancets87)).
    56	
    57	### 3. Secret Key Handling & Parsing in `issuer-decrypt-balance.ts`
    58	- **Key isolation**: Secret keys are accepted only via `WCONF_ELGAMAL_SK` environment variable or `--key-file <path>`. There is no `--key` CLI argument, protecting against `ps` inspection and shell history logging.
    59	- **Error path leakage**: Validation errors report only `key.length`; decryption failure errors output generic diagnostic text without echoing secret key material.
    60	- **Parser defects**: Trailing `--key-file` without an argument causes an unhandled `TypeError` ([Finding 4](#finding-4-medium---toolsissuer-decrypt-balancets52-93)); unknown CLI flags corrupt positional argument resolution, and `--flag=value` syntax is silently ignored ([Finding 5](#finding-5-medium---toolsissuer-decrypt-balancets65-68)).
    61	
    62	### 4. Confidentiality Claims & Prohibitions
    63	- Output strings in both tools strictly adhere to confidentiality boundaries: amounts are reported as confidential, while transaction metadata (types, timestamps, counterparties, destination tags) and aggregate issuance confidential supply are explicitly marked public. Both tools prominently log that balances are never hidden from the issuer (and auditor, where configured).
    64	- **Defect identified**: Documentation and header docstrings overclaim that `observer-harness.ts` "links no cryptography library" despite importing `@noble/curves` and `@noble/hashes` ([Finding 3](#finding-3-medium---claimsmd105-toolsobserver-harnessts11-13)).
    65	
    66	### 5. Claims E-59 and Upstream Feedback U-34
    67	- **CLAIMS.md E-59**: Claims harness self-verification and awards grade `A`. Because `observer-harness.ts` depends on an unlisted package (`ripple-address-codec`), it fails in clean environments, making Grade `A` unsupported by reproducible evidence ([Finding 1](#finding-1-high---toolsobserver-harnessts38-packagejson12-18), [Finding 3](#finding-3-medium---claimsmd105-toolsobserver-harnessts11-13)).
    68	- **UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK.md U-34**: Fairly presents the upstream trade-offs by explicitly noting that upstream packages were curated for proof generation rather than external observers. However, it erroneously classifies `EncZero` derivation as a "constant" rather than a deterministic computation ([Finding 6](#finding-6-low---upstream-feedbackmd122)).
    69

    81	#### <a id="finding-1-high---toolsobserver-harnessts38-packagejson12-18"></a>Finding 1 (HIGH) - `tools/observer-harness.ts:38`, `package.json:12-18`
    82	**Undeclared runtime dependency `ripple-address-codec` crashes harness on clean install**
    83	- **What is wrong**: `tools/observer-harness.ts` imports `decodeAccountID` and `encodeAccountID` directly:
    84	  ```typescript
    85	  // tools/observer-harness.ts:38
    86	  import { decodeAccountID, encodeAccountID } from "ripple-address-codec";
    87	  ```
    88	  Neither `package.json` nor `bun.lock` declares `ripple-address-codec`. In a clean checkout or CI environment, running `bun run tools/observer-harness.ts` throws `Cannot find package 'ripple-address-codec'`. This directly invalidates the claim in `CLAIMS.md` E-59 that the harness executes and self-verifies.
    89	- **Why it matters**: The observer harness cannot execute in reproducible environments, breaking automated verification.
    90	- **Recommended fix**: Add `"ripple-address-codec": "^5.0.0"` (or repository pinned version) to `package.json` dependencies and regenerate `bun.lock`.

## 6. The vendored prover, verbatim

`/tmp/wconf/mpt-crypto/src/utility/mpt_utility.cpp:770` — the function being wrapped. **This is the authority for Q2.**

```c
int mpt_get_convert_back_proof(
    uint8_t const priv[32], uint8_t const pub[33],
    uint8_t const context_hash[32], uint64_t const amount,
    mpt_pedersen_proof_params const* params, uint8_t out_proof[816])
{
    if (!priv || !pub || !context_hash || !params || !out_proof) return -1;
    if (amount > params->amount) return -1;
    /* ... parse pub -> pk, params->ciphertext -> (b1,b2),
           params->pedersen_commitment -> pc_b ... */
    if (secp256k1_compact_convertback_prove(
            ctx, out_proof, params->amount, priv, params->blinding_factor,
            &pk, &b1, &b2, &pc_b, context_hash) != 1) return -1;

    uint64_t const remaining_balance = params->amount - amount;
    uint8_t* bp_ptr = out_proof + SECP256K1_COMPACT_CONVERTBACK_PROOF_SIZE;
    size_t bp_len = kMPT_SINGLE_BULLETPROOF_SIZE;
    uint8_t const* blinding_ptrs[1] = {params->blinding_factor};
    return mpt_get_bulletproof_agg(&remaining_balance, blinding_ptrs, 1,
                                   context_hash, bp_ptr, &bp_len);
}
```

`mpt_pedersen_proof_params` is `{ pedersen_commitment[33]; amount; ciphertext[66]; blinding_factor[32]; }`.

⚠ **Note `params->amount` is the BALANCE and the bare `amount` argument is the public withdrawal.** The two are one letter apart in the C and mean opposite things.

/**
 * ⚠ **3001 was the first default and it collided with a Docker-hosted Gitea on
 * the development machine** — the API died with `EADDRINUSE` while the browser
 * application's proxy happily forwarded `/api/*` to the stranger, which
 * answered `303 See Other` with a login redirect. **Nothing in the tier noticed.**
 * The port moved to 3011 AND `/api/meta` now carries an identity the client
 * checks, because a free port is luck and a checked identity is not.
 */
export const DEFAULT_PORT = Number(process.env.WCONF_WEB_PORT ?? 3011);

/**
 * ⚠⚠ **LOOPBACK, EXPLICITLY — AND THE DEFAULT WAS NOT.** `listen(port)` with a
 * bare number forwards no hostname to `Bun.serve`, whose default is
 * **`0.0.0.0`**. **Measured 2026-08-28**: a `Bun.serve({port})` with no hostname
 * answered on this machine's LAN address. So `make web-api` was publishing a
 * holder's ledger view — and, while the issuer route still decrypted, an
 * unauthenticated decryption oracle — **on every interface** (`CLAIMS.md`
 * E-213).
 *
 * ⚠ **CORS was not a defence and was never going to be**: it constrains
 * browsers, and `curl` is not a browser. The comment in `vite.config.ts` about
 * binding to `127.0.0.1` was applied to the *dev server* and not to this one.
 */
export const BIND_HOSTNAME = "127.0.0.1";

export function serve(port = DEFAULT_PORT) {
  const app = new Elysia().use(api).listen({ port, hostname: BIND_HOSTNAME });
  console.log(`W-CONF read-only API on http://${BIND_HOSTNAME}:${port}/api`);
  console.log(`⚠ READ-ONLY. This process signs nothing and holds no key (ADR-0003 D1).`);
  console.log(`⚠ Bound to ${BIND_HOSTNAME} only — not reachable from another machine.`);
  console.log(`⚠ Devnet only.`);
  return app;
}

if (import.meta.main) serve();
/**
 * The **only** Unix-domain-socket client in this repository's web tier.
 *
 * Architecture §6 step 8, decision D1 (`ADR-0003`): the web tier may send
 * `Request::Status` and nothing else. `Pair` and `Prepare` are the requests
 * that carry authority, and neither is constructible from anywhere under
 * `web/`. This file is what `tools/check-web-readonly.ts` pins.
 *
 * # Why this file exists in `web/api/` and not in `web/app/`
 *
 * `signer/src/ipc.rs` is a Unix domain socket precisely so that *"a browser
 * page cannot connect at all. No web API opens a Unix socket."* That property
 * closes the XSS-to-spend-oracle path by construction. ⚠ **A server function
 * in the browser application would reach around it in one line** — which is
 * exactly what TanStack Start's server functions are for — so the socket lives
 * on the far side of an HTTP hop that the browser cannot bypass, and the gate
 * asserts that nothing under `web/app/` imports this module or names the path.
 *
 * # What `Status` actually returns, which is not what its comment used to say
 *
 * ⚠ Until 2026-08-28 `protocol.rs` documented this variant as a *"Public
 * ledger read"*. **It reads no ledger.** `session.rs`'s handler returns the
 * crate version, a hardcoded `keys_loaded`, and a note carrying the holder's
 * address, the randomness ledger's generation id and its entry count
 * (`CLAIMS.md` E-205). None of that is a secret.
 *
 * ⚠⚠ **BUT IT DOES EXCEED WHAT THE PROTOCOL PUBLISHES, AND THIS COMMENT SAID
 * OTHERWISE UNTIL 2026-09-04 — `CLAIMS.md` E-239.** `signer/src/store.rs:753-756`
 * commits a record *"on success **and** on any local failure before emission — a
 * cancelled confirmation, a failed proof, an aborted attempt"*, so the **entry
 * count includes attempts that never reached the ledger and no observer can
 * reconstruct.** P-4 and P-11 leave *who transacts and how often* public; they
 * do not publish this count, nor the local socket path this module also returns.
 *
 * ⚠ The retracted claim (E-230) surviving as a paraphrase — the shape the
 * disclosure-phrase gate cannot catch.
 */

import { tmpdir } from "node:os";
import { existsSync } from "node:fs";
import { join } from "node:path";

/** What the signer answers. Mirrors `Response::Status` in `protocol.rs`. */
export interface SignerStatus {
  version: string;
  keysLoaded: boolean;
  note: string;
}

/** Why a status read did not happen. A missing signer is not an error here. */
export type SignerProbe =
  | { reachable: true; status: SignerStatus; socketPath: string }
  | { reachable: false; reason: string; socketPath: string };

/**
 * Where the signer puts its socket, mirroring `ipc.rs::default_socket_path`.
 *
 * ⚠ Mirrored, not shared — one is Rust and one is TypeScript, so this is two
 * readings of one convention and it can drift. `signerSocketPath` is exported
 * so a test can assert the shape rather than leaving the duplication silent.
 */
export const SIGNER_SOCKET_LEAF = "signer.sock";
export const SIGNER_SOCKET_DIR_PREFIX = "wconf-signer-";

/**
 * Every directory the signer might have put its socket under, in order.
 *
 * ⚠⚠ **MIRRORING RUST'S CHOICE WAS TRIED AND IS THE WRONG APPROACH.** Round one
 * replaced a hardcoded `/tmp` with `os.tmpdir()`, and round two showed that still
 * diverges: **measured with `TMPDIR` unset and `TMP` set, Rust bound at
 * `/var/folders/…/T` while Node's `tmpdir()` returned `TMP`.** Rust's Unix
 * `std::env::temp_dir()` reads `TMPDIR` and then the Darwin per-user directory;
 * Node's reads `TMPDIR`, `TMP`, `TEMP`, `/tmp`. **They are different algorithms
 * and no amount of care makes one file predict the other** — that is two
 * readings of one convention, which is this repository's most-recorded drift
 * source (`CLAIMS.md` E-221).
 *
 * ⚠ **So this DISCOVERS rather than PREDICTS**: it tries each candidate and
 * uses the first whose socket actually exists. When none does, it reports every
 * path it looked at, which turns an invisible mismatch into a legible message.
 */
export function signerSocketCandidates(): string[] {
  const dirs = [
    process.env.XDG_RUNTIME_DIR,
    process.env.TMPDIR,
    tmpdir(),
    "/tmp",
  ].filter((d): d is string => Boolean(d));
  const uid = process.getuid?.() ?? 0;
  const seen = new Set<string>();

  // ⚠ E-241: the policy must state the WITHHOLDING, because a reader who sees
  // "no key here" may still assume the figure is served.
  test("the key policy says the decrypted figure is NOT served", () => {
    expect(ISSUER_KEY_POLICY).toMatch(/DOES NOT SERVE a decrypted balance/i);
    expect(ISSUER_KEY_POLICY).toMatch(/loopback is not uid-scoped/i);
  });

  test("it names the mirror, never a holder-keyed balance", () => {
    expect(MIRROR_FIELD_LEDGER_NAME).toBe("IssuerEncryptedBalance");
    expect(MIRROR_FIELD_LEDGER_NAME).not.toMatch(/ConfidentialBalance/);
  });

  // ⚠⚠ **THIS TEST FALSE-PASSED, AND IT WAS WRITTEN IN THE ROUND THAT FIXED A
  // FALSE PASS.** `SNAPSHOT_PATH` was a module-level `const`, bound at import
  // time, so setting the env var afterwards did nothing and `await import()`
  // returned the cached module. It read the DEFAULT path — absent in a clean
  // checkout — so `issuerView` said unavailable for that reason and the test
  // passed with the refusal DELETED. **Measured: removing both refusal lines
  // left all 46 tests green.** The path is read lazily now, and the control
  // below is what proves the assertion can fail (`CLAIMS.md` E-215).
  const withSnapshot = (body: unknown, fn: () => void) => {
    const tmp = join(REPO, `web/api/.test-snapshot-${Math.abs(hashOf(JSON.stringify(body)))}.json`);
    writeFileSync(tmp, JSON.stringify(body));
    const prev = process.env.WCONF_ISSUER_SNAPSHOT;
    process.env.WCONF_ISSUER_SNAPSHOT = tmp;
    try { fn(); } finally {
      if (prev === undefined) delete process.env.WCONF_ISSUER_SNAPSHOT;
      else process.env.WCONF_ISSUER_SNAPSHOT = prev;
      rmSync(tmp, { force: true });
    }
  };

  test("CONTROL — a clean snapshot IS read, so the refusal test below can fail", () => {
    // ⚠ Without this the refusal test proves nothing: an unavailable result is
    // the default for a missing file, which is what made it false-pass.
    withSnapshot({ producedAtUnix: 1, entries: { "a/b": { available: true, decrypted: "5" } } }, () => {
      const v = issuerView("a", "b");
      expect(v.available).toBe(true);
      // ⚠ E-241: the snapshot HAS the value; the API reports only that it exists.
      expect(v.decryptable).toBe(true);
    });
  });

  // ⚠⚠ **THE POINT OF E-241, AND THE ONLY TEST THAT FAILS IF IT REGRESSES.**
  // The decrypted figure must never cross this boundary. Loopback is not
  // uid-scoped, so a served value reaches every local user out of a file the
  // producing tool writes `mode 0600` (REVIEW-036 U1, E-228).
  test("the decrypted balance is NEVER served, even when the snapshot has it", () => {
    const secret = "123456789";
    withSnapshot({ producedAtUnix: 1, entries: { "a/b": { available: true, decrypted: secret, publicAmount: "7" } } }, () => {
      const v = issuerView("a", "b");
      expect(v.available).toBe(true);
      expect(v.decryptedWithheld).toBe(true);
      // The figure appears NOWHERE in the serialised response.
      expect(JSON.stringify(v)).not.toContain(secret);
      // ...while the public plaintext amount, which IS public, still does.
      expect(JSON.stringify(v)).toContain("7");
    });
  });

  test("CONTROL — the withholding is not vacuous: an absent value is distinguishable", () => {
    withSnapshot({ producedAtUnix: 1, entries: { "a/b": { available: true, publicAmount: "7" } } }, () => {
      expect(issuerView("a", "b").decryptable).toBe(false);
    });
  });

  test("the snapshot reader REFUSES a file carrying anything key-shaped", () => {
    withSnapshot(
      { producedAtUnix: 1, sk: "deadbeef", entries: { "a/b": { available: true, decrypted: "5" } } },
      () => expect(issuerView("a", "b").available).toBe(false),
    );
  });

  test("...and refuses one naming the secret env var anywhere in it", () => {
    withSnapshot(
      { producedAtUnix: 1, note: `produced with ${FORBIDDEN_SECRET_ENV}`, entries: { "a/b": { available: true, decrypted: "5" } } },
      () => expect(issuerView("a", "b").available).toBe(false),
    );
  });

  test("balances are DECIMAL STRINGS, so a UINT64 above 2^53 survives", () => {
    // ⚠ 18446744073709551615 is UINT64 max; Number() would render it as
    // 18446744073709552000 with no indication anything happened (E-215).
    const big = "18446744073709551615";
    withSnapshot({ producedAtUnix: 1, entries: { "a/b": { available: true, decrypted: big, publicAmount: big } } }, () => {
      const v = issuerView("a", "b");
      // ⚠ `decrypted` is withheld now (E-241), so the UINT64 property is
      // asserted on `publicAmount` — which IS public ledger data and is served.
      expect(v.publicAmount).toBe(big);
      expect(String(Number(big))).not.toBe(big); // the loss this avoids, demonstrated

## B.5 What to deliver for question B

- **Findings with severity**, each with the concrete sequence that produces the harm.
- **Corrections to A1, A2 or A3 where they are wrong** — stated precisely enough to implement from the prose alone. The standard this project applies is that a sketch fails: the finding that began the whole `r`/`ρ` rotation was *"not implementable from this text."*
- **An explicit statement of what the amended store does NOT protect against.** Prior rounds have been credited for this and it is not optional.
- **A ruling on whether the signer may be built against A1–A3 as they stand**, with or without your corrections.

## Two disclosures this packet is obliged to make about itself

**Conflict of interest, partial.** This packet was written by the session that will also adjudicate it. That conflict is standing in this project and has been disclosed every round. **One conflict was deliberately removed:** issue #9 records that the session which ruled on keygen argued three times that Option 2's risk is worse, and directs that it must not frame this question. It did not. This packet was written from a cold read of the ADR and the architecture, and §A.3 changes 2 and 3 — the two arguments that cut against the expected outcome — are its own additions, present in no prior document.

**A standing bias in this project's self-review: it finds *mechanisms* and misses *instances*.** It has three times shipped a document that was true when written and false by the end of the same session. **If you find a factual claim in this packet that is stale rather than wrong-in-principle, that is the most valuable thing you can return** — particularly any package version, any published/not-published status, or any assertion about what upstream exports. Re-check registries at review time, not from the text.
     3	One endpoint is not an acceptable confidentiality source here unless it is explicitly treated as a fully trusted ledger oracle. The reason is not generic “more decentralization is better”; it is F-23: a wrong destination or issuer key can cause a failed but applied transaction that permanently publishes the amount encrypted to the attacker.
    16	A useful third option is **local pinning/TOFU for encryption keys**, especially destination `HolderEncryptionKey` and issuer `IssuerEncryptionKey`. It is not a full substitute for corroboration because first use can be poisoned and legitimate key rotation needs handling, but it would catch later substitution and is cheap. For recurring counterparties, TOFU plus corroboration is stronger than either alone.
    20	> A single remote ledger endpoint is trusted for amount confidentiality. If it lies about the destination or issuer encryption key, the signer can create a transaction that fails but permanently publishes the amount encrypted to the attacker’s key.

   105	
   106	**Read this last, and only if you want it.** It is placed after the deliverable deliberately: an earlier draft put it before, and both review legs found the packet was steering. **You are not being asked to ratify, match or even consider this.** Per C-6 there is no interoperability reason to follow it.
   107	
   108	`XRPLF/mpt-crypto`, `generate_deterministic_nonces` (`src/mpt_internal.h:218`) at commit `85dbac7`, read from source and confirmed by execution (`CLAIMS.md` E-48, E-55). **It is hedged, despite the function's name:**
   109	
   110	```
   111	IKM  = "MPT1" ‖ k(4,BE) ‖ witness_len(4,BE) ‖ domain_len(4,BE)
   112	             ‖ witness ‖ statement_hash(32) ‖ domain
   113	PRK  = HMAC-SHA256(salt, IKM)                       [Extract]
   114	n_i  = HMAC-SHA256(PRK, prev ‖ i [‖ sub_counter])   [Expand]
   115	```
   116	
   117	- `salt` is 32 bytes from `RAND_bytes`, so output is randomness-dependent. Two proofs over an identical statement differ — verified by execution, natively and under WASM.
   118	- Each output is reduced mod the curve order; a zero result retries with an extra counter byte, capped at 256 attempts.
   119	- The 16-byte length-prefix preamble answers **TOB-RIPCTXR-10**, which upstream documents as a brittle-API concern rather than a live exploit. The `"MPT1"` tag reserves a migration point.
   120	- Under WASM, `RAND_bytes` reaches `getentropy`, which Emscripten routes to `crypto.getRandomValues` in a browser and `node:crypto` under Node.
   121	- **No exported entry point lets a caller influence the nonce** — no seed argument, no RNG hook, no global.
   122	- **No upstream test asserts that two proofs over identical statements use distinct nonce material.** That is a grep-based negative, graded C, and a second reviewer with repository access did not contradict it (`UPSTREAM-FEEDBACK` U-30).
    70	
    71	## 4. What to rule on
    72	
    73	1. **Derivation.** Deterministic from witness and transaction context, hedged as the nonces are, or fresh RNG with structure? Note `r` becomes public for Convert/ConvertBack — does that change the answer versus Send?
    74	2. **Whether `r` and `ρ` get the same treatment**, or differ.
    75	3. **Admissibility.** Wide reduction, abort-on-zero, no rejection sampling — carry over, or not, and why?
    76	4. **Failure behaviour** when the entropy source fails, if your construction uses one.
    77	5. **Domain separation** from the nonce derivation. They must not collide; say how.
    78	6. **What the signer must refuse.** Should the localhost interface ever accept a caller-supplied `r`? (§(a)'s interface is value-shaped, so it currently cannot.)
    79	7. **A detection rule.** `--assert-no-r-reuse` catches reuse *after* the fact. Is there something the signer should check *before* signing?
    80	
    81	## 5. Deliverable
    82	
    83	- A byte-exact construction, in the form the sigma-nonce ruling took: preimage layout with framing, KDF, reduction, failure behaviour.
    84	- Known-answer vectors, or a statement of exactly what blocks them.
    85	- A test that fails if the construction is not followed.
    86	- Explicit scope: what your construction does **not** protect against.

# Review packet — the last item before ADR-0001 is `Accepted`, plus the A1–A3 state machine

- **Date:** 2026-08-18
- **Two questions, one rotation.** They are unrelated to each other. Answer both; do not let one colour the other.
- **Neither is a cryptography question.** Question A is a risk judgement about where code runs. Question B is a state machine that guards a cryptographic invariant, not the invariant itself.

---

## 0. Where this project stands, in one screen

A receivables financing facility on XLS-0096 Confidential MPTs, XRPL Devnet, built as an honesty artifact rather than a deployable product. Amounts are hidden from the public; **identities, counterparties, timing and frequency are not, and the issuer can always decrypt.** The architecture is `docs/architecture/ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md` and it is explicitly provisional.

**Every cryptographic packet is now closed.** §(a)'s threat model is ruled (a hostile same-host process is in scope). §(e)'s sigma nonces are ruled, implemented and executed on Devnet. §(e)'s keygen half is ruled. `r` and `ρ` are ruled. Three of ADR-0001's four preconditions are met.

**One agenda item keeps ADR-0001 at `Proposed`, and it has survived three revisions without anybody being asked to do it.** That is question A. Question B is bundled because it is the last review gate in front of the signer and nobody scheduled it either.

---

# QUESTION A — re-weigh Option 2 (browser WASM) on risk alone

## A.1 What was decided, and on what

ADR-0001 §(a) asked where proof generation runs. Producing a `ConfidentialMPTSend` needs the holder's ElGamal **secret** `sk_A`, because the compact sigma proof links the balance commitment to the on-ledger ciphertext via `B₂ − b·G = sk_A·B₁` (E-33, Grade A, read in full). Whoever generates the proof holds the spending key. Three options:

| | Secret lives | Can spend | Can decrypt | Build cost |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Server-side proving** | Application server | Server | Issuer, auditor, server | Low |
| **2. Browser WASM** | Browser memory | Holder + anyone who compromises the page | Issuer, auditor | High |
| **3. Local holder-side signer** | Holder's machine | Holder | Issuer, auditor | Medium |

**Option 3 was chosen.** Option 2 was rejected on **two** grounds, stated in one sentence: *"Option 2 has the worst risk profile and the highest cost: because holder keys cannot be rotated or recovered, a single page compromise is permanent."*

## A.2 Why it is being re-opened

**Rev 3 (2026-08-16) found that the cost ground had collapsed and explicitly deferred the re-weigh:**

> *"the comparison table below now overstates Option 2's cost, and the review rotation must re-weigh it on risk alone rather than inheriting a cost argument that no longer holds."*

Rev 3, rev 4 and the keygen rotation have all passed. **No reviewer has ever been asked.** The decision is probably right; half its stated justification is retired. That is the shape of thing this project treats as unfinished, and it is the entire remaining distance between ADR-0001 and `Accepted`.

## A.3 Three things changed since the table was written. They do not all point the same way.

**This packet's job is to put all three in front of you, including the ones that argue for Option 2.** A re-weigh that only surfaces the evidence supporting the incumbent is not a re-weigh.

### Change 1 — the keygen ruling makes Option 2's risk worse. Against Option 2.

REVIEW-012 (2026-08-18) ruled that `sk_A` is **derived from the XRPL master seed** — specifically the raw 16-byte family seed, with the seed type and AccountID bound into the preimage.

- **Before:** the ElGamal key was an independent secret. A page compromise cost confidentiality of amounts. It did not touch spending authority.
- **After:** to derive `sk_A`, whatever generates proofs must hold **the root credential of the XRPL account.** Under Option 2, that is browser memory. A single page compromise now yields spending authority *and* retroactive decryption of the entire confidential history — permanently, with no rotation and no recovery (E-9, E-10, P-10).

The keygen ruling names this itself: master-seed compromise is *"the accepted cost of the ruling, not an oversight."* Option 3 accepts that cost against an adversary who must achieve code execution on the holder's machine. Option 2 would accept it against an adversary who must compromise a web origin.

### Change 2 — the cost ground did not "largely" collapse. It inverted. For Option 2.

Rev 3 understated this, and the ADR has not been updated since:

- **`@xrplf/mpt-crypto@0.1.0` is published and works** (E-53, Grade A, executed). Hex-in/hex-out TypeScript over a vendored WASM build, zero runtime dependencies. It exports all four proof generators, all four context hashes, ElGamal encrypt/decrypt, Pedersen commitments and homomorphic add/subtract.
- **`ripple-binary-codec@2.9.0` serialises everything** — all five transaction types, all 18 confidential fields, verified against a live Devnet node's `server_definitions` across all 381 fields with zero mismatches (E-30, E-46).
- **The JS client path is three published packages** — `@xrplf/mpt-crypto` + `ripple-binary-codec@2.9.0` + `ripple-keypairs` — with **no patching, no vendoring and no build step**. A `ConfidentialMPTConvert` whose every piece of proof material came from the npm package was accepted and validated on Devnet on 2026-08-17.

Meanwhile **Option 3's cost went up, materially, and by ruling.** The issue #1 threat-model ruling withdrew rev 2's pasted-token design and obliged: per-transaction confirmation rendered in the signer's own UI from the transaction the signer itself built; short-lived scoped authority; no browser-persisted bearer secret; OS-mediated IPC or native messaging preferred over localhost HTTP. That ruling says so in its own words — *"this is materially more build than the alternative, and it was chosen knowing that."* Option 3 also needs a vendored and patched C fork, an FFI binding, a Rust binary, and a distribution story it does not have.

**So the build-cost column is not merely stale in Option 2's cell. The ordering may have inverted.** On present evidence Option 2 is the cheapest path and Option 3 is the most expensive. **You are asked to weigh risk alone — but you are entitled to know that the cost argument now runs the other way, because that is exactly what makes this a real question rather than a rubber stamp.**

### Change 3 — a rescue for Option 2 that this packet is obliged to surface. Ambiguous.

Keygen condition 2 requires that, for accounts carrying `lsfDisableMaster`, a regular key or a signer list, the signer **offer an explicitly configured standalone ElGamal seed with its own backup.** That path exists and is ruled.

**If it were made general, change 1 evaporates.** A browser holding a standalone ElGamal seed does not hold the root credential, and Option 2's risk returns to what it was when the ADR rejected it.

You are asked to say whether that rescues Option 2 or merely relocates the problem. Points on both sides that this packet is not resolving for you:

- A browser-held ElGamal seed is still unrotatable and unrecoverable. Page compromise is still permanent, just narrower.
- Making the standalone path general discards §(e)'s only benefit — recoverability from a backup the holder already keeps — and reintroduces "a second thing to lose", which is the outcome §(e) was chosen to avoid.
10:## 1. Question A — ruling: **Option 3 confirmed, on risk alone.**
12:**Both legs rule Option 3, independently, and neither treats it as a formality.** The re-weigh is discharged.
17:2. **Option 3's adversary needs same-user code execution on the holder's machine**, and must additionally defeat a confirmation rendered by a separate process. Both legs size this as multiple privilege tiers, not a marginal step.
20:**Neither leg's ruling depends on the cost column.** Both were told the cost column had inverted, both re-derived it against Option 2, and both still ruled Option 3. That is the property the packet was written to test.
30:**The argument survives in a sharper form, and the sharper form is the one the ADR must now carry.** The keygen ruling's effect on Option 2 is not that spending exposure is new. It is that:
33:- **It welds a rotatable exposure to an unrotatable one.** An XRPL signing key can be retired — `SetRegularKey`, `lsfDisableMaster`. `sk_A` cannot, at any level (P-10). After the keygen ruling both descend from one credential, and the unrotatable half governs.
45:**Option 3 is now the most expensive option and was chosen anyway.** The ADR must say that, because a table claiming Option 3 is cheap is a claim a reader can falsify in an afternoon.

- `docs/reviews/REVIEW-027-REQUEST-b21-and-e171-2026-08-27.md:394` includes the **complete 359-line `src/hazmat.rs`**.
- That crate is **BSD-3-Clause**. The first packet contains neither copyright nor licence text. The proposed NOTICE supplies neither. [Crate licence](https://docs.rs/crate/ed25519-dalek/2.2.0/source/LICENSE).
- Markdown also reproduces rippled code and substantial specification text: examples are `docs/reviews/REVIEW-003-REQUEST-2026-08-16.md:59` and `docs/reviews/REVIEW-021-REQUEST-step7-convert-back-2026-08-25.md:139`. Upstream rippled is ISC-licensed; XRPL-Standards is MIT-licensed. These need inclusion in the quotation/attribution inventory.

Findings:

- **R1-1 | MEDIUM | NOTICE’s third-party inventory is incomplete | D:91,150–153; REVIEW-024:1803,1859; REVIEW-027:394; byte comparisons against the downloaded crate | Add the BSD copyright, conditions and disclaimer; inventory and provide applicable notices for the other reproduced source and specification excerpts.**
- **R1-2 | LOW | D3 mixes line-count definitions and gives an unreproducible benchmark denominator | D:85–90,137–140; `git apply --numstat` and explicit line comparisons above | Publish the counting command and definition; use 500 removed/341 context/756 added, distinguishing blank lines.**

I searched the **643 tracked files**, including TypeScript, Rust, JSON/vector generators, Markdown source excerpts, web components, generated routing code and CSS. The tracked inventory contains no image, font, icon or vendored `.js` asset files. The XRPL vectors inspected identify committed generators rather than copied upstream KAT datasets.

I did **not** establish provenance for every unmarked line, compare every dependency’s complete source tree, or resolve the legal treatment of every quotation.

A′ does its narrow attribution job: it explicitly disclaims relicensing the identified C material. It supplies **no redistribution permission**. The surrounding stack’s ISC licensing and the project’s useful upstream contributions do not establish permission or quantify enforcement risk. That limitation is acknowledged in D3, so I am not reporting it as newly discovered. [GitHub’s no-licence guidance](https://choosealicense.com/no-permission/).

**Seam 2 — rename and pre-push guard**

GitHub’s quoted rename behaviour is accurate. Old Git URLs redirect after rename; reusing the old repository name ends that redirect. The sibling repositories’ visibility and creation dates also match D4. [GitHub rename documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/creating-and-managing-repositories/renaming-a-repository).

Local experiments with Git **2.50.1** established:

| Route | What happened / design consequence |
|---|---|
| `url.<base>.insteadOf` | Hook received the **rewritten destination**, not the original alias |
| `remote.origin.pushurl` | Hook received the **push URL** |
| `--no-verify` | Push succeeded without invoking the hook |
| `core.hooksPath` override | Push succeeded without invoking the installed hook |
| Fresh clone | Repository-local hook was absent; push succeeded |
| Linked second worktree | Shared the hook and was refused; a second worktree is **not automatically a bypass** |
| `gh repo create --source . --push` | CLI source delegates to Git push; it does **not inherently bypass** an effective hook |
| Remote `gh repo sync` | Uses GitHub APIs, so no local hook runs; this does not establish that unrelated private objects would be accepted |
| IDE | A normal Git invocation normally runs the hook; an alternative Git implementation or overridden configuration needs separate verification |

Sources: [Git hooks](https://git-scm.com/docs/githooks), [CLI create implementation](https://github.com/cli/cli/blob/trunk/pkg/cmd/repo/create/create.go), [CLI sync implementation](https://github.com/cli/cli/blob/trunk/pkg/cmd/repo/sync/sync.go).

The decisive experiment was a push from an unhooked toy clone into a repository with an unrelated public root:

```text
mixed_push_exit 1
main_rejected True
private_branch_published True
```

**`git push --all` rejected `main` but successfully created another branch containing the toy private history.** Main-branch protection and a failed push exit code therefore do not establish confidentiality.

- **R1-3 | HIGH | A repository-local URL hook does not close the private-history publication route | D:181–188,267–270; bypass and mixed-result push experiments above | Remove public-repository write authority from private-history environments; reserve publication credentials for the isolated export. Keep the hook as an additional guard, preferably allowing only the canonical private destination.**

A URL expression must account for owner/repository case, optional `.git`, trailing slash, scp-style SSH and `ssh://` forms. It should inspect Git’s effective destination, not reconstruct it from `origin`. A blacklist remains weaker than a private-destination allowlist.

One qualification to the proposed test: after the rename, the old URL can still resolve to the renamed private repository. Thus testing before public creation can invoke the hook. Require its **specific refusal marker**; an authentication or repository-not-found error is not proof that it ran.

**Seam 3 — copying issues**

The stated citation counts reproduce with the case-insensitive pattern **`issues? #[0-9]+`**:

- **119 files; 390 matches**
- #1: **157**
- #10: **70**
- #11: **50**

The literal singular pattern produces different counts; the plan should preserve the actual expression.

The restricted issue queries confirmed:

| Measurement | Result |
|---|---|
| Issues | **13** |
| Closed/open | **10 / 3** |
| Open numbers | **5, 8, 10** |
| Comments | **17 total** |
| Pull requests | **0** |
| Withheld IDs in bodies | #6: **U-28**; #7: **U-31**; #10: **U-32** |
| Withheld IDs in comments | #2: **U-36**; #3: **U-37**; #4: **U-35, U-38** |
| Titles containing withheld IDs | **0** |
| Other title ID | #7 contains **U-24**, a public row |

The titles concern subjects already described in the public tracked material; I found no title-specific leak.

Issues, PRs and Discussions must all be kept out of the initial numbering sequence. Transfers and integrations that create them also matter. A push, workflow run, label or release does not itself reserve an issue number. This workflow has read-only permissions and no creation step. Enabling Discussions can lead through creation of a welcome discussion, so defer that setup too. [GitHub Discussions setup](https://docs.github.com/en/discussions/quickstart).

Creating the repository privately is useful, but **privacy alone does not reserve numbers**. Assert each returned number as it is created, and stop immediately on a mismatch.

The semantic question remains **uncleared**. The allowed queries establish that #5 and #8 have no `U-nn` references and #10 has one U-32-bearing paragraph. They cannot establish that other paragraphs paraphrase no withheld observation. I did not evade the transcript restriction to read those bodies.

Findings:

- **R1-4 | MEDIUM | The publishable issue payloads and semantic-redaction gate are not concretely bound | D:234,271–275; open issues have three comments between them, while step 9 scans “13 bodies” | Specify title plus sanitized body only, explicitly exclude comments/attachments, complete the semantic review, and create each issue from the exact swept file. Freeze those files rather than re-fetching bodies during creation.**
- **R1-5 | MEDIUM | The universal stub promise is false | D:238–240; REVIEW-013 request:15,161 still presents the unresolved re-weigh, whose final disposition is in `docs/reviews/REVIEW-013c-adjudication-option-2-and-amendments-2026-08-18.md:10` | Give each closed stub a verified outcome-document link; remove the promise that every citing document contains the outcome.**

Step 9 does not explicitly authorize copying comments, but “full copies” invites that interpretation. If comments are copied, the specified body-only sweep has not covered the complete publication payload.

**Seam 4 — execution order**

I rehearsed the export, added the drafted NOTICE/README reference, initialized Git, committed, performed D2’s two commits, and committed step 6’s README changes. Installation succeeded:

```text
156 packages installed
227 pass
5 skip
0 fail
ci: every step passed
```

That was macOS verification. The sweep component reported **97/97 controls** and **PASS-SHAPES-ONLY**. It was not the full local-store publication gate.

The commit arithmetic is unavoidable:

| After step | Commit count |
|---|---:|
| 4 | **1** |
| 5, first commit | **2** |
| 5, regeneration commit | **3** |
| 6, literally executed without an additional commit | **3, with a dirty README** |
| 6, README committed as evidently intended | **4** |

Squashing the rehearsed tree back to one commit caused the revision-log gate to fail:

```text
FAIL: 739b29f is not an ancestor of HEAD.
```

The log embeds the hash of the commit introducing `CLAIMS.md`; putting that generated log into a replacement root invalidates its anchor.

Findings:

- **R1-6 | HIGH | Steps 4–6 cannot produce the one-commit publication they promise | D:252–264,271–272; rehearsal above; `tools/build-claims-revisions.ts:765,812` | Resolve the bootstrap design before execution: either change the promise to an approved short bootstrap history, or adapt the revision-log format/gate to support a genuine single root. Merely squashing afterward fails.**
- **R1-7 | HIGH | Step 7 does not seal the complete object graph or the final published tree | D:259–278; `tools/check-no-secrets.ts:1200–1203` disables history scanning under `--rev`; step 6 lacks a commit instruction and step 9 recommends later workflow edits | Finish and commit every file change first; require a clean worktree; validate the intended history/ref set and scan export history strictly; record the approved HEAD and push only that object. Re-run after any workflow pinning edit.**
- **R1-8 | HIGH | A literal required check named `ci` will never complete | D:276; `.github/workflows/ci.yml:6,16` names the workflow `ci` but its job `checks` | Require the emitted check named `checks`, or rename the job. Select and verify the actual emitted context from the staging run.**
- **R1-9 | MEDIUM | The first Linux verification is observed only after irreversible publication | D:275–280; workflow uses `ubuntu-latest`, while the recorded/rehearsed executions are macOS | Wait for the private staging repository’s first CI run to pass before changing visibility.**

GitHub uses the **job name** for a workflow’s required status check. [Required-check naming documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/troubleshooting-rules).

There is **no settings-availability finding** in the stated order: step 9 makes the repository public before enabling private vulnerability reporting and branch protection. Those features are available for public repositories on GitHub Free. Moving both settings earlier would encounter availability restrictions. [Branch protection](https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-protected-branches), [private vulnerability reporting](https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/how-tos/report-and-fix-vulnerabilities/report-privately).

The tested CI path needs no access to the private repository. Historical commit citations deliberately remain unverifiable to outsiders; step 6 discloses that limitation. They remain an evidence-access cost, not a runtime dependency.

**Seam 5 — surface corrections and E-322**

The requested upstream reads confirmed `status: Final` at **`33ea4ca66`**, **`58ccd3047`** and **`master`**. PR #604 merged on **2026-09-03**. [Pinned specification](https://github.com/XRPLF/XRPL-Standards/blob/33ea4ca66/XLS-0096-confidential-mpt/README.md).

The corrected API descriptions agree with current code:

- `web/api/server.ts:109–123` removes `socketPath` and the note.
- `web/api/issuer.ts:180–193` returns `decryptable`, not `decrypted`.
- A request-level probe using a **synthetic scratch snapshot** returned HTTP 200, `decryptable: true`, no decrypted field, and no occurrence of the synthetic figure.
- `/api/meta` no longer described the disclosure as unruled.

Two defects remain:

- **R1-10 | MEDIUM | A newly added README sentence is false | `README.md:31` says XLS-0096 was Draft for most of this project’s life; the first commit is 2026-08-16 and Final was set 2026-09-03—approximately 18 of 63 days by review day | Replace “for most of this project’s life” with the two dates.**
- **R1-11 | MEDIUM | E-322’s sweep left active copies of the removed disclosure state | `docs/explainer/WEB-TIER-STACK-AND-PHILOSOPHY-2026-08-28.md:34–35`; `docs/architecture/ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md:235`; launch plan:137–140; `tools/check-disclosure-phrases.ts:126,138` | Date the historical descriptions and state that E-241 removed the figure, count and path. Preserve records, but mark their supersession where readers encounter them.**

The ADR’s contradictory status prose and “currently accepts” paragraph also remain at `docs/decisions/ADR-0003-web-tier-trust-boundary.md:3,352`. E-322 explicitly acknowledges those; acknowledgement does not make their current-tense statements true.

I found no remaining **unqualified current claim** outside the exempt historical/register material that withheld rows will remain in public history. The explainer’s Draft wording is explicitly dated to its September 1 reading and now records Final afterward; that is a historical statement, not a surviving current-status error.

**The single change I would make before step 1:** replace the hook’s role as the confidentiality boundary with **credential isolation: private-history environments cannot write to the public repository; only the isolated, verified export environment can publish.**
